HORNER ON ALGEBRAIC TRANSFORMATION. 

(Concluded from page 37.) 

40. Having thus endeavoured to shew that some important portions of the 
Algebraic and Differential Theories are only exemplifications of a general 
theorem deduced from the principles of algebraic division, I will close these 
remarks by briefly describing how the basis of both theories may, by the 
same simple means, be completed. 

The reader will have remarked that the more usual differential formulas 
are those, whose coefficients occupy diagonal lines in the table of art, 33, 
etc.^ and consequently in the general table in art, 13. The slightest 
valuation from the principle [art, 11) on which that table was constructed 
would have produced the formulae in question. It was only requisite to 
stop the division by making p = m, one term earlier in each course than in 
the next preceding. The effect of this alteration may very easily be intro- 
duced into the formulae in art, 13, by referring to what was shewn in a 
preceding article respecting the cotemporary change effected by division, in 
the unknown variable of one term and the coefficient of the next. For in 
reversing this effect, it is obvious that any number of the latter terms of a 
transformee may be removed into a subsequent one without altering its 
value, if we observe to change the coefficient of the first of the transplanted 
terms for that which belongs to its new situation. 

41. Thus Mq ;r -|- No being changed to Mj a? 4" Nq, this may be substi- 
tuted for the last two terms of the second equation ; which will then end in 
LJa?;iri+Mia?+No. These terms by merely changing Li for Lg may be put 
for the last three terms of the third equation ; and so on. The effect of 
continuing this process through n transformees is obvious, and may be thus 
stated : — 

If we take the terms which descend diagonally from Nq changing Xp for 
Xp^u t^ constitute a formula =/)r. The same applies to the diagonal 
descending from any other absolute. Hence we have

PROPOSITIONS ON THE CONIC SECTIONS 
[Mr. James DalmcLhoi/, Edinburgh,] 

(Continued from p. 63, No. 2., Vol. II.) 
PROPOSITION VII. THEOREM. 

If from any point in the curve of an ellipse or hyperbola two chords 
be drawn, and parallel to these respectively two semidiameters ; the 
sector included between the semidiameters is equal to half the sector 
having for its base the portion of the curve intercepted by the diverging 
chords. 

From D, any point in the curve of an ellipse, draw two chords DA and 
DB ; and respectively parallel to these draw the semidiameters CE and CF : 
the sector ECF is equal to half the sector ACB. 

For produce EC and FC to meet the curve 
in E', F'; then since AD is parallel to EE' 
and BD to FF, the sector DCE = ACE and 
DCF = BCF; therefore DCE— DCF = 
ACE — BCF; but DCE — DCF= ECF 
= ECF ; and ACE —BCF = ACB— ECF ; 
hence ECF = ACB — ECF, and ECF = 
ACB 

2 ' 

In the hyperbola the proof is the same, but the chords are supposed to 
terminate in opposite branches. 

PROP. VIII. THEOREM. 

If through any point in the plane of an ellipse or hyperbola two lines 
be drawn, each meeting the curve in two points, and two semidiameters 
be drawn parallel respectively to these lines; the sector included 
between the semidiameters is equal to half the sum or half the difference 
of the sectors having for their bases the portions of the curve interested 




PROPOSITIONS ON THE COKIC SECTIONS. 133 

hy the two diverging lines, according as the point of divergence of 
those lines is within or without the curve. 

1. Through H, any point within the ellipse, let two chords AHD and 
BHG be drawn, and parallel to these respectively draw the semidiameters 
EC and KC ; the sector KCE is equal to half the sum of the sectors ACB 
and DCG. 

For join the points B, D, and parallel to BD draw the semidiameter FC ; 
then by the last proposition, the sector KCF is equal to half DCG, and 
the sector ECF is equal to half ACB ; therefore KCF + ECF = ECK 
__ ACB+DCG 
"" 2 

2. Through L, any point without the ellipse, draw two chords AG and BD, 
and parallel to these respectively the semidiameters MC and FC : the sector 
FCM is equal to half the difference of the sectors DCG and ACB. 

For draw AD, and parallel to it the semidiameter EC ; then by the last 
proposition, the sector ECM is equal to half DCG, and the sector ECF is 

equal to half ACB ; therefore FCM = ECM — ECF = ^^^""^ — . 

PROP. IX. THEOREM. 

(Corollary to Desargue's Theorem,) 
If a conic section be described about each of the simple quadrilaterals 
of which a complete quadrilateral is composed, and a transversal be 
drawn intersecting the three curves and the four sides of the quadri- 
lateral, these intersections will, by Desargue's theorem, furnish three 
systems of points in involution, and the centres of any two of these 
systems are conjugate points with respect to the centre of the third 
system. 

Lemma, Let A, B, C, D be any four points lying in a straight line, and 
let 

A BCD

FP.PT = AP.BP ; PF.P'F = AP'.CF ; PP'.FP' = AP'.DP^

(AP +FP)(CP — FP) = (BP + FP)(DP — FP) ;

therefore FP = ^aP-BP) + (DP~CP) '

•' (AP— BP)-(DP— CP)* 

And if these values of FP and P'P be multiplied together, the result, after 
reduction, is 

FP.P'P = AP.BP.

MATHEMATICAL NOTE. 

[jBy a Gentleman Cadet.'] 

The fundamental formula for the sine of the sum of two arcs may be very 
simply deduced in the following manner. 

Let ABC be a plane triangle, and draw CM perpendicular to the side 
AB ; then sin C = sin (A + B). 

Now AB.CM = BM.CM + AM.CM = twice 
triangle ABC, 

and, AC.BC sin C = twice triangle ABC. 

Equating these expressions and dividing by 
AC.BC, gives 

. CM BM , AM CM 

''''^-AC-BC+ AC'BC' 
or, sin (A-|-B)= sin A cosB+cos A sin B.

This is analogous to the theorem Ad quatuor lineas of the ancients,

Jfamj quadrilateral he described in a conic section^ any transversal

When the three points R, R', Q', and the surface (3) are given, the points

Hence if four of the intersections of four variable planes move on a 
given surface of the second degree^ and if three of tlie planes porss

Bkianchok. — See the Mathematician, Vol. I., p. 289, art. 29.

ELEMENTARY INVESTIGATION OF THE FORMULA FOR 
COMPUTING THE EQUATION OF EQUAL ALTITUDES.

a scientific friend, and may perhaps interest some of the readers of the

LXII. Mr. James Reid, Prior's Salford, 
If two parabolas be described on the same axis so that the vertex of the 

second may be the focus of the first, and its parameter half that of the other ; 

then if from the points in which normals of the smaller parabola meet the 

evolute of the larger, as centres, and the respective normals as radii, circles 

he described, these will all pass through a certain fixed point. 

Remar'k, — For ** respective normals as radii," read, parts of the normals 

intercepted between the evolute and the smaller parabola as radii. 
[First Solution. By Geometricus,] 
Let ABC, A'B'C be the two parabolas, the vertex A' of the latter 

coinciding with the focus of the former. Take any point B in the parabola 

ABC, and draw BB' parallel to the axis A A' intersecting the parabola 

A'B'C in B' ; draw also the normals BP, B'P 

at the points B, B' meeting the axis in V, V, 

and each other in P. Draw the tangent BT, 

and the ordinales BR, B'R', and let PQO be 

drawn perpendicular to the axis intersecting 

it in Q and meeting BB' produced in O. 

I^astly, draw A'S perpendicular to BT, and 

join A'B, A'B' and A'P. 
' Then the subnormal RV =: 2 A A', and the 

parameter of ABC being double that of A'B'C, 

the subnormal R'V = A A' ; hence RV = 2R'V'. 

NowBir-=:i4AA'.AR, andB'K'-=2AA'.A'R'; but BR'^rrB'R'^ there- 
fore A'R'= 2AR ; consequently 

BB' = KR'=:RA'+A'R'=:RA'+2AR=:AR+AA'= A'T=A'B=A'V, 
since TBV is a right angle ; hence also the chord A'B' is parallel to the 
tangent BT and perpendicular to the normal BP. But the normal BP 
makes equal angles with the lines A'B and BB', and since it has been shewn 
that A'B = BB' ; therefore it is evident that PA' = PB', and a circle 
described with centre P and radius PB' will pass through A', the vertex of 
the parabola A'B'C. 

Since AS intersects the tangent BT in the tangent AS at the vertex, we 
have A'S^- = AA'.A'T and A'B^ = A'T^ ; therefore A'B*; A'S^ : : A'T or

It may be remarked in passing that the consideration of (4) shows us that

circle intersects the parabola in the point of contact.

LXIII. From the Putney College Examination PaperSy July, 1845. 
The ground plan and picture being taken as planes of projection, there 
are given two points, and a finite line by means of their projections, and a 
plane by means of its traces^ If light emanate from one of the given points, 
and the given line enlightened by it cast a shadow on the given plane, it is 
required : — 

( 1 ) To construct the projections of the shadow on the planes of projection. 

(2) To construct the perspective of that shadow, the eye being in the 
second given point. 

[Solution. Mr. C, H, Brown, Student, C. C. E.] 

The solution is readily effected by means of the two following elementary 
problems, which are given in all works on Descriptive Geometry, and 
which it is sufficient to merely quote as Lemmas, See, for instance, Le- 
fehure de Fourcy, TraitS de Giomitrie Descriptive, probs. i. and iii. 
pp. 44 and 49. 




=.^^ 



Lemma 1 . Given the projections of two points in a line, to construct the 
traces of that line. 

Lemma 2. Given the projections of two points in a line, and the traces 
of a plane, to construct the projections of the intersection of the line and 
plane. 



156 SOLXTTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 

Take A as the luminous point, BC the given line, and E the place of the 
eye, respectively denoted by their horizontal projections fl„ biCi^ e^ and their 
vertical projections 02? ^2^2* ^2 &gt; and let P1PP2 be the given plane denoted by 
its traces, PPi and PPj- 

Now the shadow of the extremity B of the given line is the interse(5tion of 
the straight line AB through the luminous point A and the point U, Let 
this intersection be found (Lemma 2) and its projections on the two planes 
be ^1^2- Similarly let 7172 the projections of the intersection of AC with the 
plane P1PP2 be found. 1'hen ^171 will be the horizontal projection of the 
shadow, and )3a72 t^he vertical. The projections of the shadow are therefore 
found. 

To find the perspective of that shadow, we have only to find where lines 
drawn from its extremities to the eye pierce the vertical plane of projection, 
which in the problem is the picture plane. But we have the horizontal 
projections e^fii and ^171 and the vertical projections 62^2 and ^272 5 and hence 
(Lemma 1 ) we find the vertical traces p^qi of those lines. The line p.^2 is 
the perspective required. 

Note. In this sketch the eye is taken on the opposite side of the picture 
plane from the other data, as in perspective should be the case. Divested, 
however, of the technical application of the problem, the data may have any 
position whatever : but in such case, the line which represents the perspective 
might not fall within the limits of the picture. 

Good solutions were sent by Messrs. Godfrey and Hearn.

LXV. Mr, Fenwick, 

Prove that the locus of the centres of the innumerable surfaces of the 
second order that have eight points common in space, is a surface of the 
second order, and thence deduce the corresponding locus in piano. 

The locus in space, it will be seen, is the intersection of two surfaces of 
the second order, that is, two hyperboloids of one sheet (see page 81.) — S.F. 

[Solution. M7\ Tliomds Weddle.] 

Let X = . . . . ( I ) X'= .... (2) be the equations of two of the 
surfaces passing through the eight points, then will 

X + \X' = (3) 

represent all the surfaces, X being arbitrary ; let also 

U=:0, V = 0, W = (4) 

and U'=0, r=0, W'=:0 (5) 

be the equations for determining the centres of (I) and (2) respectively. 
Then will 

U + XU' = 0, V + X V = 0, W + \W' = (6) 

determine the position of the centre of (3), and the elimination of the 
variable X will give the locus of the centre. The elimination being effected, 
we have 

UV — U'V=:0, UW — U'W=:0, VW — V'W = 0....(7) 

and any one of the equations (7) is a consequence of the other two. We 
hence infer that in general the locus is a curve which is the intersection of 
two surfaces of the second order. 

The preceding investigation will apply to curves of the second order 
passing through four points in a plane, if (1, 2) represent two of the 
curves, and those equations be rejected which contain VV or W. We shall 
find finally, UV — U'V = 0, from which we see that the locus is a curve 
of the second order. 

We may observe that the locus would be a surface of the third order, if 
the surfaces were restricted to pass through seven points only. 

It may not perhaps be much out of place to notice the following theorem 
here, as it is somewhat connected with the above, as well as with various 
discussions in the " Mathematician." 

Let diameters conjugate to a given plane he drawn to the innumerable 
surfaces of the second order passing through eight points in space j the 
locus oftliese diameters is a surface of the second order. 

Let U = 0, V = 0, and U' = 0, V = 0, be the equations to the diameters 
of(l) and (2) respectively, which are conjugate to the given plane; then 
will the diameter of (3) conjugate to the given plane be denoted by 

U + XU' = 0, V + XV' = 0. 

The elimination of X gives UV — U'V = 0, for the locus which is there- 
fore a surface of the second order.

The double sign arises from the generality of the investigation, as if the

angle formed by producing C A, BA ; wherefore the positive sign without 
the vinculum must be taken.

than {nii^+mj^), or z is less than ^\/ {2(mi'+tW2*)). It follows, therefore, 
that the negative sign within the vinculum must be used.

LXIX. Mr. J. IF. Elliott, Greatham. 

If from a point P in a conic section, a tangent PO be drawn to meet the 
axis major AB in O, and the chords AP, BP to meet a perpendicular 
through O in Q, Q' : then QO = OQ'. 

[First Solution. Mr, H, Godfray, David Places St. Mark's Road, Jersey ; 
and similarly by Mr. Thomas Weddle, Newcastle.] 

This theorem admits of greater generality than as proposed in the 
exercise ; it may be thus enunciated : — If from a point P in a conic section, 
a tangent PO be drawn to meet any diameter AB in O, and the chords 
AP, BP to meet a conjugate to AB in Q, Q' ; then QO = Q'O. 

For draw the ordinate PM, then (Davies's Hutton^ II, page 1 38, cor. 1 , 
for the ellipse, and page 164, cor. I, for tlie hyperbola) AB is divided 
harmonically in M and O : 

.-. AM : AO : : BM : BO. 
But by similar triangles, 

AM : AO : : PM : QO, and BM : BO :: PM : Q'O ; 
.-. QO = Q'0. 
The same property holds for the parabola by considering the chord 
through A to be another diameter through P. 

The solution of Mr. Samuel Bills, Hawton, was analogous to the 
preceding, AB being the major axis, as in the exercise.

LXXII: Mr, Rutherford, 

If three equal forces be represented in magnitude and direction by the 
three lines drawn from the angular points of a plane triangle to the centre 
of the circumscribed circle, their resultant will be represented in magnitude 
and direction by the straight line which joins the centre of the circumscribed 
circle and the point of intersection of the perpendiculars from the angles on 
the opposite sides. 

[First Solution. William BurnSy L,L,D.f Rochester.^ 

Let ABC be the triangle, O the centre of the circumscribed circle, P the 
point of intersection of the perpendiculars AD, BN ; and draw OA, OB, 
OC, OP. Draw also OH, OK perpendicular to BC, CA, and join HK. 
Since AK = KC, and CH = HB, therefore HK is 
parallel to AB, and AB equal to 2HK. Also the tri- 
angles APB and HOK being equiangular, BP = 20K. 
Hence if the force OC be resolved into forces represented 
in magnitude and direction by OK, KC, and OA into two 
represented by OK, KA ; then KC, KA being equal and 
opposite, 20K = BP is the resultant of OA, OC. Also 
if OB be resolved into the two OP, PB, then since BP 
and PB are equal and opposite, OP is evidently the direction and magnitude 
of the resultant of the three forces OA, OB, OC. 




SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 169 

[S ECON D Solution. Mr, Thomas Weddle ; and similarly hy Messrs, S. Bills, 
and W, H, Levy, Shalbourne. ] 
Draw OH and AD perpendicular to BC : on AD take AP = 20H, and 
draw OP ; then P is the point of intersection of the perpendiculars from 
A, B, C on the opposite sides (Mathematical Companion, No. V, page 48). 
Now the force OA is equivalent to the forces PO and PA ; OC to OH and 
HC ; and OB to OH and HB ; hence the three forces OA, OB, OC are 
equivalent to the forces PO, PA, 20H, HC and HB acting at O. Of these 
PA and 20H are equal and opposite, and so are HC and HB ; hence the 
forces OA, OB, OC are equivalent to the single force OP, that is, the force 
OP is the resultant of the three equal forces OA, OB, OC. 

[Third Solution. Mr, J. W, Elliott, Chreatham, Stockton-on-Tees; and 

similarly by Messrs, J, Reid, Prior^s Salford; H, Godfray, Jersey ; 

B. Harley, Northtoich; and H,B,L,'\ 

Let ABC be a plane triangle, O the centre of its circumscribed circle, and 
AO, BO, CO the lines representing the three equal forces in magnitude and . 
direction. Draw OHR perpendicular to BC, and make OR = 20H ; then 
OR represents in magnitude and direction the resultant of BO, CO, as is 
i¥ell known. 

Again, complete the parallelogram AORP, and draw the diagonal OP, 
which will represent in magnitude and direction the resultant of the forces 
OA, OR, that is, of the three forces AO, BO, CO. 

• It now only remains to show that P is the point of intersection of perpen- 
diculars from A, B, C upon the opposite sides. 

Since CO = OB, OR bisects the angle COB and intersects BC at right- 
angles in H; and therefore AP being parallel to OR by construction, 
coincides with a perpendicular from A upon BC. Moreover AP=OR 
=:20H ; hence P is the point of intersection of perpendiculars from A,B,C. 
Vide Lady's and Gentleman's Diary, for 1 843, p. QO, 

[Fourth Solution. By JDunelmensis,]

80. By Juvenis. 

A merchant bought a cask of spirits for £48, and sold a quantity exceeding 
three-fourths of the whole by two gallons at a profit of £25 per cent. He 
afterwards sold the remainder at such a price as to clear £60 per cent by the 
whole transaction ; and had he sold the whole quantity at the latter price he 
would have gained £175 per cent: find the number of gallons contained 
in the cask. 

I^ote. This exercise is to be solved by the principles of arithmetic. 
81. Mr. B. Harley^ JsTorthwich. 

From each angle of a quadrilateral A BCD inscribed in a circle let a line 
be inflected to the opposite diagonal, so that it and the other diagonal may 
make equal angles with the adjacent sides : then will the areas of the 
triangles ABC, ADC, ABD, BCD, into which the quadrilateral is divided 
by its diagonals, be to each other respectively as the four inflected lines. 

82. By Geometricus. 
Given the sum of the two sides of a plane triangle, the radius of the 
inscribed circle, and either of the angles at the base, to construct the triangle. 

83. Mr. James Beid, Prior's Salford. 
If from the point in which a normal to an ellipse meets the axis major a 
straight line be drawn at right angles to the normal, the part of either of the 
focal distances intercepted between this line and the curve will be an 
harmonic mean between the focal distances.

certain distance from the base, and h feet farther off it subtended an angle of 
0° ; find the height of the column and distance of the observer from it at first.

There are given three lines SP, SQ, SR and two points A, B, all in the 
same plane. Let any triangle PQ H be described having its angular points upon 
the three given lines, and two of its sides passing through the given points ; 
then, whatever be this triangle, its third side will pass through a given point.

In every tetrahedron, the sum of the squares of the six edges is equal to 
four times the sum of the squares of the lines which join the middles of the 
opposite edges. 

[Our readers are requested to furnish any other properties of the figure, 
either new or little known, in order to the formation of a general collection 
of such properties.] 

87. Mr. Thomas Weddle. 

An ellipse can be divided geometrically into n equal sectors when n in 
such, that a regular polygon of n sides can be described geometrically : 
required proof. 

%S. Mr. Fenwick. 

The equation in general of a surface of the second order when referred to 
any three coordinate planes, involves nine independent coefiUcients ; if, how- 
ever, the planes of reference be tangential to the surface, three of the nine 
coefficients will be functions of the remaining six. It is required to express 
the equation of the surface in terms of the six coefficients.

91. Mr. G. W. Hearn, B. M, College. 

A wire ARM bent into the form of a spiral moves in a vertical plane about 
the pole C, the machine being so counterpoised that C is also the centre of 
gravity of the revolving mass ; CR is a fixed vertical straight rod on which 
a small ring R slides, the spiral wire also passing through the ring. Find 
the form of the spiral and other circumstances, that the vertical motion of the 
ring may be uniform.

A rough horizontal plane is constrained to revolve round a vertical axis 
with a constant angular velocity, and a uniform cylinder is placed with its 
base upon it ; if the friction be sufficient to prevent sliding, find the ratio of 
the diameter of the cylinder to its height, so that it may just upset.

PROPERTIES OF THE ELLIPSE. 
[Mr, Thomas Weddle, Newcastle on Tyne.] 

CONJUGATE DIAMETERS. — DIVISION OP AN ELLIPSE INTO EQUAL 
SECTOBS. — MAXIMUM INSCKIBED, AND MINIMUM CIRCUMSCRIBED 
POLYGONS. 

Upon AB, the major axis of an ellipse ACBD, whose centre is 0, 
describe a circle (the figure is easily conceived), and from any point P in 
the ellipse draw PM perpendicular to AB meeting the circle in Q. Let 
AO = a, CO = by and &lt; QOB = 0, then will a; = OM = a cos 0, and 

y = PM = QM = b sin 0, hence the equation to the ellipse may be put 

under the simple form 

x =. acos0, j/ z=zb sin &lt;/&gt; • (1) 

The introduction of the eccentric anomaly (as is called in astronomy) 
into the equation of the ellipse, will frequently be found advantageous ; and 
it is one of the objects of this paper to show how greatly it simplifies the 
investigation of many properties of this important curve. 

Throughout this paper the point Q^ in the circle will correspond to 
Pm in the ellipse, and 0,» will denote the value of at these points. 
The equation to the chord Pi P2 is therefore 

y — &amp; sin0i ar — a cos0i 

^(sin0i — sin02) "" a(cos0i — cos02)'

ON TANGENT PLANES. 
[Mr. James Reid, Prior's SalfordJ] 

In the Mathematician, Vol. II. p. 83, a method of finding the tangents of 
plane curves is given. In this paper the same principle is applied to the 
investigation of tangent planes. 

If jt?, q and r are any whole positive numbers whose sum does not exceed 
?^, a surface of the %*^J order is represented by the equation 

^(kpqrXPy^Z^):=zO (I) 

Since through any given point in the surface innumerable plane curves 
may be traced upon the surface, and a rectilineal tangent drawn to every 
curve, the number of such tangents at the given point is infinite. As we 
are not at liberty to assume that more than two of the tangents lie in one 
plane, we shall first determine the plane of two tangents, and then prove 
that it is the locus of all. 

Let the equation to a plane passing through a point [xyz) of the 
surface be 

Aa?4-B2^ + C5?+D = (2) 

Remove the origin in (I) and (2) by substituting a?i + iP, ^i + y, ^1 + « 
for a?, y, z. 

By the binomial theorem

and this is precisely the condition necessary and sufficient in order that the

Therefore, of the three rectilineal tangents to the curve surface which lie 
in the three coordinate planes, the plane which contains two contains also

which contains any two tangents contains every other that can be drawn at 
that given point.

of the variables vanish. In this case {xi, y^ Zi) is a singular point, and 
the tangents do not lie in one plane.

ON THE THEORY OF COLLINEATION. 

(Gontinaed from page 128.) 
iMr. Rutherford.] 

VIII. 

It may be interesting to shew how the equations expressing the relation 
existing between the coordinates of a point in the one system, and those of 
a corresponding point in the collinear system are deduced from geometrical 
considerations. 

Let Ay B, C, D be four points in a straight line, 
in one system, and A',B', C three points in a straight 
line in the other system. Place the line ABCD ^ 

making any angle with the line A'B'C so that A 
may comcide with A'. Draw BB', CC, and let ^ 

them be produced and meet in P : join PD inter- ^-^ 

secting A'B'C in D' ; then D' is the point of the ^ b^^^^n 

latter system which corresponds to the point D of 
the former system. 

For the point D is the point of intersection of the ^ ? — S ? p 

lines ABC and PD in one system, and conse- 
quently in conformity with the definition of collinear systems, the point 
which is to correspond to D must be in the intersection of A'B'C and PD, 
which is therefore the point D'. 

From this construction, the relation between the coordinates of a point in 
one system and those of a corresponding point in the other system may be 
determined analytically in the following manner.

(!.) If straight lines be drawn from the angles of a triangle through 

anypoint in the plajie of the triangle to meet the sides, and the lines 

joining these points of intersection a?id the sides of tJie triangle be 

produced to meet, the three points of intersection will be in the same 

straight line. 

Let ABC be the triangle (see last figure), and O the point in its plane 
through which and the angular points of the triangle, the lines AOA', BOB', 
COC are drawn. Also draw A'l)', B'C, C'A' and let them be produced 
and meet the sides AB, BC, AC in P, R, S; then will the points P, R, S 
be in a straight line. 

For the point O can be regarded as the collinear centre, and A', B', C as 
points corresponding respectively to the points A, B, C : therefore AB, A'B'; 
BC, B'C; AC, A'C' are corresponding lines ; and since (v) corresponding 
lines intersect in the collinear axis, the three points of intersection P, R, S 
must be situated in the collinear axis PRS, and therefore they are in a 
straight line. 

(2.) From each of three points in tlie same straight line draw two 
straight lines so as to form, by tlieir mutual intersections, three quadru 
laterals ; the diagonals of these quadrilaterals, three and three, (viz. 
one diagonal of each quadrilateral), will pass through the same point. 

Let O, Q, Q' be three points in one straight line, and from each of these 
points let two lines be drawn so that, by their mutual intersection, the four 
quadrilaterals ABCD, 
A'B'C'D', A'B'B'D' 
may be formed ; then 
shall the diagonals 
AC, A'C, WD' pass 
through the same 
point P'. 

For the lines join- 
ing Q,Q'; A, A' and 
D,D' pass through the 
same point 0; and 
therefore to the points 
Q,Q' the points A, A' 
as well as the points 
D,D' may be taken as 
corresponding points ; 
hence the lines QA, 
Q'A', are correspond- 
ing lines, and also the 
lines QD, Q'D'. Con- 
sequently the points B, B' and C, C which are situated in the intersection of 
OBC with those lines, may again be taken as corresponding points ; and 
therefore AC, A'C and Bl), B'D' are corresponding lines ; but since cor- 
resi)onding lines of collinear and collinearly situated figures, intersect in the 
collinear axis (v) ; therefore the corresponding lines QA, Q'A' ; QD, Q'D' ; 
AC, A'C ; BD, B'D' must all intersect in one line, the collinear axis of the 
systems ABCD and A'B'C'D'. But QA, Q'A' intersect in B", and QD, Q'D' 
in D ' ; hence the two corresponding lines AC, A'C, as well as the two other 




ON THE THEORY OP COLLINEATION. 193 

corresponding lines BD, B'D' must intersect in the collinear axis B "D" ; 
therefore the diagonals AC, A'C, B'D" tend to the same point P' in the 
collinear axis B"D". Again since B, B' and D, D' are corresponding points, 
the corresponding lines BD and B'D' must intersect also in the collinear 
axis B"D". In a similar manner it may be shown that the diagonals AC, 
BD, B'D' pass through one point, and the diagonals A'C, A'C, BD pass 
through one point : therefore the truth of the theorem is established. 

We have therefore determined /bwr new points in which three straight 
lines constantly intersect, and therefore these points may be taken as collinear 
centres, and we may determine other lines which intersect three and three 
in one point. 

Hence from the last theorem it follows that if the three collinear centres 
of three collinear figures^ which are two and two collinearly situated^ 
are in the same straight line, the three collinear axes intersect in the 
same point, 

(3.) Three points are situated in a straight line, and lines are drawn 
from these points to any arbitrary point : from two of the given points 
draw lines to any point in the line parsing through the arbitrary point 
and the third given point, intersecting the two lines drawn through the 
arbitrary point and the two specified points ; then the line drawn 
through the two points of intersection will always pass through a fixed 
point in the straight line in which the three given points are situated. 

Let A, A', A' be three points in a straight 
line, and P any arbitrary point ; draw the lines 
PA, PA', PA", and let Q be any point in the 
line PA". Let the lines drawn through Q 
and the two points A, A' intersect PA and 
PA' in B and B' ; then the line joining B, B' 
will pass through a fixed point O in the line 
AAA". 

For A, A' and B, B' can be regarded as 
corresponding points of two systems, and the 
point of intersection (O) of the lines joining 
these corresponding points as the collinear 
centre. Now since A and A' are on opposite 
sides of the point O, the principle in (tii) will 
here be applicable ; therefore AB', A'B are corresponding lines as well as 
AB, A'B', and they must therefore intersect in the collinear axis ; PQA" is 
consequently the collinear axis. Take any point C in PA and draw 00 
intersecting PA' in C ; then and C are corresponding points, and hence 
AC and A'O are corresponding lines; these lines must then intersect in a 
point Q' in the collinear axis, and therefore, conversely, if from any arbitrary 
point Q in the line PA" two lines be drawn through A and A', meeting PA 
in B and PA' in B', the line joining the points of intersection B and B' will 
always pass through a fixed point in A A', which is the collinear centre of 
the two systems. 

In this maijner a variety of propositions may be readily demonstrated, and 
it will be seen that the collinear theory applies very happily to a large class 
of interesting and elegant theorems.

XXXII. 

Upon any three lines in space, tw two of which are in the same 
plane, nor all three parallel to the same plane, a parallelopiped may 
he constituted having these lines as three non-contiguous and non- 
parallel edges ; ' and there can he only one such parallelopiped con- 
stituted on the same three lines. 
LetAB',BC', 

CA' be the three \ 

given lines, un- 
limited in their 

extensibility, no 

two of which are 

in the same 

plane nor all 

three parallel to 

the same plane. 
Through AB' 

one plane, and 

one only, can be 

drawn parallel 

to BC; and 

throughBC'one 

plane, and one 

only, parallel to 

A B'. These 

planes, also, as 

is well known, 

are parallel to 

each other. In 

like manner, 

through BC'and 

C A' a single pair 

ofparallel planes 

may be drawn ; 

and through 

CA', AB' ano- 
ther single pair. 
The three 

pairs of parallel 

planes thus con- 
stituted will by 

their mutual in- 
tersections form a parallelopiped, having AB', BC, CA' for non-contiguous 

and non-parallel edges. 

One such parallelopiped can therefore be always constituted on the three 
lines, as stated in the proposition. Also, since the pairs of parallel planes 
through the lines AB', BC, CA' are unique, the parallelopiped formed by 
them is, of necessity, unique.

Draw the diagonals AA', BB', CC, DD' of the parallelopiped : these, as 
is known, all meet in a point, P, and bisect each other there. Then, of the 
parallel edges to the three given lines, those which join the extremities of 
the same diagonals (DD' being, for the present excluded) have this pro- 
perty i-- of each resting upon two of the directrices of the curve surface 
generated by a line moving along them^ and being parallel to the third 
directrix. Thus, B'C rests upon AB' and CA' and is parallel to BC ; 
C'A rests upon AB' and BC and is parallel to CA' ; A'B rests upon BC 
and C A' and is parallel to AB'. These are the three lines referred to in the 
foot note to art. xxviii : and they have received the name of symmetrical 
edges ; so that AB', BA' are symmetrical to one another, as are likewise 
BC' and C'A symmetrical respectively to B'C and CA'. 

The six edges in question form a twisted hexagon, (Jiexagongateche) 
whose opposite sides are respectively equal and parallel. The alternate 
sides are successively a directrix and a generatrix ; and these are inter- 
changeable in virtue of art. xxx. 

xxxiir. 
If the six edges of the twisted hexagon^ formed as in the preceding 

paragraph^ be cut by any plane, the six points of intersection will lie 

in a conic section.

Draw rtP : then since B'C, BC are parallel they are in one plane ; and 
P being in that plane, rtP is so likewise, and will hence cut B'C in a point a'.

XXXV. 

Since A'B rests upon the two directrices A'C, BC and is parallel to the 
third AB' it is one of the generatrices meeting the third directrix at an 
infinite distance. It is therefore situated upon the hyperboloid. In the 
same manner, B'C and C'A are also generatrices of the hyperbolo'id : and 
they form an unique system. 

Now any three of the generatrices may be taken as directrices, as has been 
shewn in (xxx), and hence if we make the interchange in the case before 
us, AB , B'C, C'A will become the three corresponding generatrices, forming 
also the corresponding unique system. 

It, therefore, follows that for every generatrix of the first mode, dbCy there 
is one of the second mode, a!h'c\ parallel to it and symmetrically situated. 
From this we see that the surface is altogether symmetrical with respect to 
the parallelopiped formed upon its three defining directrices as non-conti- 
^ guous and non-parallel edges ; and that the surface has a centre, P, with 
respect to which it is perfectly symmetrical. 

Again, since for every line ahc of the first genesis there is another olIIc' 
parallel to it in the second, the plane diametral sections along any one of 
the generatrices, drawn through the centre, is composed of two indefinitely 
extended parallel lines, equidistant from the centre. 

Moreover, if any three new directrices be taken, which are in positions 
consistent with the original ones (that is, which would have given the 
surfaces, or which is the same thing, would be generatrices of the second 
system founded on the first), the point P will remain unaltered, though the 
angular points and the edges of the resulting parallelopiped will be altered 
by this change. For the six edges will be symmetrical and parallel in the 
same manner that the two corresponding ones abc, a'b'c' were proved to be. 

XXXVI. 

Instead of the parallelopiped on DD' let us conceive that another is con- 
stituted on dd, these points being on DD' produced or not, and equidistant 
from P, and the planes through d, t^' parallel to those through D, D' 
respectively: then this new parallelopiped will be similar and similarly 
situated to the original one, and the six edges which constitute the new 
twisted hexagon will be parallel to the corresponding ones of the original 
hexagon, and similarly situated with respect to the diagonal. 

The triangles in which the planes through d and d' meet the cutting plane 
will have their sides parallel to those of afiy, a'fi'^' respectively, and will 
have/? for their pole. They will also approach or recede from apy, a'p'y by 



MODEBK GEOMETBT. 



203 



equal quantities estimated on ap, Pp, &lt;^p respectively ; and hence the entire 
figure composed from these triangles will he similar and similarly situated 
with respect to ap, Pp, 7/?, whatever be the length of dd'. Whence the 
conic sections which pass through the six intersections of the edges of the 
variable twisted hexagon are always similar and similarly situated with 
respect to pa, pfi, p^. 

Again, from very similar reasoning it follows that if the original twisted 
hexagon be cut by any plane parallel to that which contains a/37, the conic 
section through the intersections of that plane with the six edges will always 
be similar and similarly situated with respect to the pole and its corresponding 
radiants. 

XXXVII. 

The preceding properties are intended to shew that there are several aspects 
under which the surface, distorted as it at first appears, becomes possessed 
of regular features. Before proceeding to shew that all the generatrices 
intersect the plane in the conic section already determined, it may be worth 
while to add one other important property indicative of the regularity of the 
surface. 

Take any one of the twisted quadrilaterals into which the surface is 
divided hy the directrices and generatrices : the line which bisects its 
diagonals always passes through the centre, P, of the parallelopiped 
upon DD'. 

Let FGHK be the quadrilateral, 
whose diagonals are bisected in M 
and N respectively : it is to be 
shewn that MN will pass through 
the centre, P, of the parallelopiped 
described on three directrices of the 
hyperboloid of one sheet, of which 
either FG,KH or KF, GH are 
two. 

Through FG, KH draw the paral- 
lel planes RV, ST ; and through 
KF, GH the parallel planes RS, 
VT. These pairs of planes are, as 
is well known, unique. 

Now the lines FH, GK which 
form the diagonals, (lying between the parallel planes RS, VT, and being 
likewise bisected in M and N), the points M,N are situated in a plane tu 
equidistant from BS, VT and parallel to them. 

In the same way it is shewn that the points M, N are situated in a plane 
rv equidistant from RV, ST and parallel to them. 

Whence, being in two planes which are respectively parallel to the two 
pairs of planes, they are in the intersection of ut, rv. 

But RV, ST are two faces of the parallelopiped depending on FG, KH as 
directrices in one mode of genesis, and hence the plane rv passes through the 
centre P of that parallelopiped. In the same manner KF, GH being taken 
as two of the directrices of the second system, the plane tu passes through 
the centre of the corresponding parallelopiped, which is the same with the 
former one P. Both planes, therefore, pass through P ; and hence their 
intersection MN also passes through P, as aflSrmed in the enunciation.

An example or two of the application of this theory to the integration of 
partial difl^erential equations might be given ; but as I am afraid of pay pap^r 
\)oiug too long, I shall here terminate it.

LXXVII. Mr. James Beid, Prior's Salford, 
If in each of the straight lines which form the edges of a tetrahedron any 
two points be taken equally distant from the middle points of the edges, the 
twelve points lie in a surface of the second order. 

[Solution. Mr, G, W, Hearn.'] 

I have shewn in my solution to the analogous plane problem proposed by 
Mr. Rutherford, that on makmg a conic section pass through four of the 
points in AB, AC, the coefficient of xy remains to be determined, and that 
on passing the curve through either of the points in BC, the same value of k 
is found. 

Let a, ft, c be the halves of three contiguous edges of the tetrahedron ; 
m, n^p the distances of the points from the middle points of the edges. 

E E 



200 



MODERN OEOMETUT. 



Draw the diagonals AA', BB', CC, DD' of the parallelopiped : these, as 
is known, all meet in a point, P, and bisect each other there. Then, of the 
parallel edges to the three given lines, those which join the extremities of 
the same diagonals (DD' being, for the present excluded) have this pro- 
perty : — of each resting upon two of the directrices of the curve surface 
generated by a line moving along them, and being parallel to the third 
directrix. Thus, B'C rests upon AB' and CA' and is parallel to BC ; 
C'A rests upon AB' and BC and is parallel to CA' ; A'B rests upon BC 
and C A' and is parallel to AB'. These are the three lines referred to in the 
foot note to art. xxviii : and they have received the name of symmetrical 
edges ; so that AB', BA' are symmetrical to one another, as are likewise 
BC' and C'A symmetrical respectively to B'C and CA'. 

The six edges in question form a twisted hexagon, (hexagon gauche) 
whose opposite sides are respectively equal and parallel. The alternate 
sides are successively a directrix and a generatrix ; and these are inter- 
changeable in virtue of art. xxx. 

XXXIII. 

If the six edges of the twisted hexagon^ formed as in the preceding 
paragraph^ be cut by any plane, the six points of intersection will lie 
in a conic section.

xxxiy. 

Let any generatrix ahc he taken with respect' to the directrices 
AB', BC, C A', resting upon them at the points a, ft, c : then another a'b'c 
may he made to rest upon their symmetrical directrices B'C, C'A, A'B, 

• The property now deduced is only a particular case of a more general theorem : — 
if ttoo triangles be copolar y their mutual intersections will be six points in the same conic section. 

For two copolar triangles are coaxial {Button, ii. p. 221.) Whence by their mutual 
intersections they give six points situated in the same conic section, by the converse of 
Pascal's theorem. 

This proof does not, however, apply in the extreme case in the text, since the axis is 
thrown infinitely distant. Whence the necessity for the modified demonstration given 
above.

Also, since aP, ftP, ^P are in the plane drawn through the point P and line

Also, since B'C, BC are equal and parallel, the triangles CPB', CPB are

XXXV. 

Since A'B rests upon the two directrices A'C, BC and is parallel to the 
third AB' it is one of the generatrices meeting the third directrix at an 
infinite distance. It is therefore situated upon the hyperboloid. In the 
same manner, B'C and C'A are also generatrices of the hyperboloid : and 
they form an unique system. 

Now any three of the generatrices may be taken as directrices, as has been 
shewn in (xxx), and hence if we make the interchange in the case before 
us, AB , B'C, C'A will become the three corresponding generatrices, forming 
also the corresponding unique system. 

It, therefore, follows that for every generatrix of the first mode, dbc^ there 
is one of the second mode, a'b'c y parallel to it and sjmametrically situated. 
From this we see that the surface is altogether symmetrical with respect to 
the parallelopiped formed upon its three defining directrices as non-conti- 
^ guous and non-parallel edges ; and that the surface has a centre, P, with 
respect to which it is perfectly symmetrical. 

Again, since for every line abc of the first genesis there is another a'b'c' 
parallel to it in the second, the plane diametral sections along any one of 
the generatrices, drawn through the centre, is composed of two indefinitely 
extended parallel lines, equidistant from the centre. 

Moreover, if any three new directrices be taken, which are in positions 
consistent with the original ones (that is, which would have given the 
surfaces, or which is the same thing, would be generatrices of the second 
system founded on the first), the point P will remain unaltered, though the 
angular points and the edges of the resulting parallelopiped will be altered 
by this change. For the six edges will be symmetrical and parallel in the 
same manner that the two corresponding ones abc^ a'b'c' were proved to be. 

XXXVI. 

Instead of the parallelopiped on DD' let us conceive that another is con- 
stituted on dd^ these points being on DD' produced or not, and equidistant 
from P, and the planes through d^ d' parallel to those through D, D' 
respectively: then this new parallelopiped will be similar and similarly 
situated to the original one, and the six edges which constitute the new 
twisted hexagon will be parallel to the corresponding ones of the original 
hexagon, and similarly situated with respect to the diagonal. 

The triangles in which the planes through d and d' meet the cutting plane 
will have their sides parallel to those of a^7, a'/3 7' respectively, and will 
have/? for their pole. They will also approach or recede from a/87, a/3V by 



MODEBIf GEOMETET. 



203 



equal quantities estimated on op, pp^ yp respectively ; and hence the entire 
figure composed from these triangles will be similar and similarly situated 
with respect to ap, pp, 7/?, whatever be the length of dd'. Whence the 
conic sections which pass through the six intersections of the edges of the 
variable twisted hexagon are always similar and similarly situated with 
respect to pa, pp, py» 

Again, from very similar reasoning it follows that if the original twisted 
hexagon be cut by any plane parallel to that which contains a/37, ^^^ conic 
section through the intersections of that plane with the six edges will always 
be similar and similarly situated with respect to the pole and its corresponding 
radiants. 

XXXVII. 

The preceding properties are intended to shew that there are several aspects 
under which the surface, distorted as it at first appears, becomes possessed 
of regular features. Before proceeding to shew that ail the generatrices 
intersect the plane in the conic section already determined, it may be worth 
while to add one other important property indicative of the regularity of the 
surface. 

Take any one of the twisted quadrilaterals into which the surface is 
divided hy the directrices and generatrices : the line which bisects its 
diagonals always passes through the centre y P, of the parallelopiped 
upon DD'. 

Let FGHK be the quadrilateral, 
whose diagonals are bisected in M 
and N respectively : it is to be 
shewn that MN will pass through 
the centre, P, of the parallelopiped 
described on three directrices of the 
hyperboloid of one sheet, of which 
either FG,KH or KF, GH are 
two. 

Through FG, KH draw the paral- 
lei planes RV, ST ; and through 
KF, GH the parallel planes RS, 
VT. These pairs of planes are, as 
is well known, unique. 

Now the lines FH, GK which 
form the diagonals, (lying between the parallel planes RS, VT, and being 
likewise bisected in M and N), the points M,N are situated in a plane tu 
equidistant from BS, VT and parallel to them. 

In the same way it is shewn that the points M, N are situated in a plane 
rv equidistant from RV, ST and parallel to them. 

Whence, being in two planes which are respectively parallel to the two 
pairs of planes, they are in the intersection of ut, rv. 

But RV, ST are two faces of the parallelopiped depending onFG, KH as 
directrices in one mode of genesis, and hence the plane rv passes through the 
centre P of that parallelopiped. In the same manner KF, GH being taken 
as two of the directrices of the second system, the plane tu passes through 
the centre of the corresponding parallelopiped, which is the same with the 
former one P. Both planes, therefore, pass through P ; and hence their 
intersection MN also passes through P, as aflSrmed in the enunciation.

[Thxbd Solution. Mr, Rutherford.] 

Let ABC he a triangle, and AiBiCi, A2B2C2 two inscribed triangles, each 
being collinjear and collinearly situated with respect to ABC. Since AiBiCi 
and A3B3C3 are similar and similarly situated, by construction, they are 
therefore collinear and collinearly situated triangles ; hence ABC and A3B3C3 
are collinear and collinearly situated triangles. Again A2B2C2 is inscribed 
in ABC and collinear and collinearly situated with it, and ABC is similarly 
inscribed in A3B3C3 ; hence A2B2C2 and A3B3C3 are collinear and collinearly 
situated triangles, and consequently their corresponding sides will necessarily 
intersect in the collinear axis, PQ, of these two systems. (See p. 192, Ex. L)

LXXVII. Mr, James Beidy Prior's Salford, 
If in each of the straight lines which form the edges of a tetrahedron any 
two points be taken equally distant from the middle points of the edges, the 
twelve points lie in a surface of the second order. 

[Solution. Mr, G, W, Hearn."] 

I have shewn in my solution to the analogous plane problem proposed by 
Mr. Rutherford, that on makmg a conic section pass through four of the 
points in AB, AC, the coefficient of xy remains to be determined, and that 
on passing the curve through either of the points in BC, the same value of k 
is found. 

Let a, J, c be the halves of three contiguous edges of the tetrahedron ; 
m, n,p the distances of the points from the middle points of the edges. 



214 SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATIOAL EXERCISES. 

Then taking the edges for axes of reference, let there be a surface whose 
equation is 

"When ;2? = 0, the coefficient of xy may be determined so that the curve 
may pass through the six points in the plane xy. When a? = 0, the coeffi- 
cient of zy may be determined so that the curve may pass through the six 
points in zy : and when y = 0, the coefficient of xz may be determined so 
that the curve may pass through the six points in xz. Hence the surface 
will pass through all the twelve points. 

Mr. Weddle gives a good solution, shewing that the twelve points in general lie in a mrface qf tht 
tccond order.

LXXIX. Professor Hearuy B. M, College, Sandhurst, 
On a rough board inclined at a given angle to the horizon a rough ball 
is projected horizontally : required its path and a confirmation of the result 
from actual experiment. 

[Solution. Mr. Heam, the Proposer,] 
The following principles are assumed as the basis of calculation. 
1st. That a ball may describe any path by rolling on a plane, provided 

the axis of instantaneous rotation passes through the centre of curvature of 

the path at every instant. 

2nd. That when the dimensions of the ball are small compared with the 
radius of curvature of the path, the axis of instantaneous rotation may with- 
out sensible error be regarded as parallel to the plane and at right angles to 
the path.

prevent sliding.

which is the same as when there is no friction. 

Hence we conclude as follows, that the actual path has an osculation of the 
third order with the parabolic path described, cseteris paribus, on a smooth 
plane, and sensibly coincides with it for a considerable extent, but that 
afterwards the horizontal range in the path rvith friction falls sensibly short 
of the range in the path without friction. But although the form of a 
part of the path described is sensibly the same in both cases, the velocity 
with which it is described will be sensibly different. Thus on reference to 
(6) we see that gain of vis viva of motion of translation without friction is to 
its gain with friction as 1 -{-/ll : I or 7 : 5. 

All these conclusions I have fully verified by experiment, and my object in 
proposing the problem was to call the attention of students to a mode of 
investigation in cases of the kind pursued by Mr. Earnshaw and subsequently 
by Mr. Walton. 

See Eamshaw's Dynamics, p. 280, 3d edition, and Walton's Mechanical 
Problems, p. 444. The problem proposed in those works is as follows :— « 
A circular board has a motion of rotation round a vertical axis passing through 
its centre perpendicular to its plane, it moves with uniform angular velocity, 
and a ball is laid upon it ; supposing friction to prevent sliding, it is required 
to shew that the path of the ball in free space is a circle concentric with 
the centre of the board. 



218 SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 

This has always seemed to me to be a strange result, and quite at variance 
with experience ; but it will be seen that the authors to whom I refer 
produce a still stranger result; namely, that with certain initial conditions 
the patli may be a circle not concentric with the centre of the board. 

I will here give my opinion concerning their mode of investigation. 

The friction is resolved in the directions of x and y and taken proportional to 

This mode of investigation therefore comes to this, — That the friction acts 

obliquely to the path, and may be resolved into /* --5- tangential to the path, 

(tt 

and fi perpendicular to it, v being the velocity, and p the radius of cur- 

r 

vature. But friction and every other resisting force always acts contrary to 
the motion, and therefore tangentially to the path described. It seems 
therefore to be explained how these authors have come to such strange con- 
clusions. If their method be applied to the problem here proposed, the 
equations of motion are 

d^x ^ (Py . d^y 

and the path described is the parabola whose equation is 

x^ = 4(/[t + i) H cosec Ly, 
which makes the horizontal range with friction greater than that without 
it, in the ratio of \/l+.'* : 1 or \/7 : \/5. I am satisfied that this is not 
borne out by experiment, 

I will merely add, that I have treated Mr. Eamshaw's problem by the 
method pursued in this paper, and have found in general a spiral path 
receding from the centre of motion, and that for certain initial conditions both 
the relative and absolute paths are logarithmic spirals. 

LXXX. By Juvenis. 
A merchant bought a cask of spirits for £48, and sold a quantity exceeding 
three-fourths of the whole by two gallons at a profit of £25 per cent. He 
afterwards sold the remainder at such a price as to clear £60 per cent by the 
whole transaction ; and had he sold the whole quantity at the latter price he 
would have gained £175 per cent: find the number of gallons contained 
in the cask. 

[FnisT Solution. Henri Mondeux^ the Shepherd o/Touraine,'] 

•«• This wonderful French youth havingr lately visited Jersey to exhibit his extraordinary powers of 
mental calculation, the above exercise was proposed to htm, and a nearly instantaneous answer was 
given. At the request of our valued correspondent, Mr. Godfray, he wrote out his solution, of which 
the following translation has been sent us by that gentleman. 

He sells the first portion at a profit of £25 per cent, and the last at £175 
per cent, and gains £60 per cent on the whole. The first profit is less than 
the mean profit by £35 per cent, and the second is greater by £115 per 
cent ; he has therefore sold 115 parts of the first against 35 of the second ; 

1 15 

that is, the first portion sold 'syas the r-^ of the whole cask, and the last the 

loU

35 3 

r~- ; but the first portion was — of the cask, and 2 gallons more, and the 
loU 4 

115 23 3 I 1 

difference between r-rrj or -^ and — is -g^ ; therefore 2 gallons is the — 

of the cask ; that is^ the cask contained 120 gallons. 

The solation of Mr. James Beid was analogous to the above. 

[Second Solution. Mr. Thomas Wilkinson, CJieapside, Burnley,'] 
Here, as 100 : 60 :: £48 : £28 j = the gain on the whole transaction. 

Again, 100 : 25 :: f of £48 : £9 = the gain on f of the whole. 

And 100 : 25 :: 2 galls. : | gal. = the gain on 2 gallons. 

Also 100 : 175 :: I of £48 : £21 = the gain on J of the whole at the 
latter price. 

And 100 : 175 :: 2 galls. : 3| galls. = the gain on the 2 galls, at the 
latter rate, therefore the whole gain is £8+£2l+J gall. — 3J gall., which 
is =£281. 

Consequently £30 — £28i=£li is the price of 3 gallons. 

Hence, £U : £48 :: 3 gall : 120 gallons, the content of the cask. 

[Thibd Solution. William Burns, L,L,D,, Roc?iester, and Mr, 
G. W. Heam,'] 

As 100 : 175 :: £48 : £84 ; hence £84 divided by 4 or £21 is the gain 
on one-fourth of the number of gallons, if sold at £175 per cent profit. 

As 100 : 25 :: £48 : £12 ; hence three-fourths of £12 is £9, the gain on 
three-fourths of the number of gallons, if sold at £25 per cent profit ; there- 
fore the whole gain would have been £30. 

But 100 : 60 :: £48 : £28*8, the actual gain; hence £1-2 is the difference 
of gain on 2 gallons at £175 and 2 at £25 per cent, or the gain on 2 gallons 
at £150 per cent profit. 

Lastly, 100 : 150 :: £48 : £72, the gain on the whole at £150 per cent., 
therefore £1*2: £ 72 : : 2 gallons : 120 gallons, the number in the cask.

LXXXII. By Geomeiricus. 

Given the sum of the two sides of a plane triangle, the radius of the 
inscribed circJe, and either of the angles at the base, to construct the triangle. 

[First Solution. Mr. Hugh Godfray, David Place, St, MarKs Road, 

Jersey.] 

Let DEF be the given circle touching the line CB in D ; draw the radius 
OD, and at the point O make the angle DOE equal to the supplement 

of the given angle. Through E draw the tangent w 

line CH intersecting CB in C : take CH equal to /*' y/i\\ 

the given sum of the sides, and draw the lines / yv j \ \ 

CO, OH and also KOI at right angles to the bisect- iVK / / \ *J 

ing line CO cutting CH in I. Describe a circle ^&lt;f ^^ / \ / 

through H, I, O determining the point B in BC. xK^ Cj\ / V 
Draw the tangent BA, and ABC will be the tri- y^^^^^^^\^ ^Qtt^ ...--''' \ 
angle required. c djc b jd 

For draw OB and OA ; then the three angles COB, OCB, OBC are toge- 
ther equal to two right angles ; and the angles OAB, OBC, OCB are obvi- 
ously together equal to a right angle ; hence OAB together with a right 
angle are equal to COB ; but COB is equal to COK and KOB, that is, 
COB is equal to a right angle together with AHB (since AHB is equal to 
KOB) ; therefore OAB or OAC is equal to AHB ; hence HB is parallel to 
AO, and therefore ABH is equal to AHB, and AB to AH : consequently 
CA and AB are together equal to CH, the given sum of the sides. There 
will be two, one or no solutions according as the circle HIO cuts the line 
CB in two, one or no points. 

[Second Solution. W. Bums, L.L. D., Classical, Commercial, and 
Mathematical School, St, Margaret's Banh, Rochester ; and in a similar 
manner by Mr, R. Harley, Northwich, Cheshire.^ 

Describe the given circle DEF touching the line CB in D, and let O be 
its centre ; draw OD and make the angle DOE equal to the supplement of 
the given angle at the base, and through E draw a line CH touching the 
circle and intersecting CB in C : take CH and CL, each equal to the sum of 
the sides, and draw HL and HO. On HO describe a segment of a circle 
containing an angle equal to the angle CLH and let it intersect CL in B : 
draw BA touching the circle DEF in F, and ABC is the triangle required. 

For draw OB, OC, and BH, ; then since BD and BF are tangents to the 
circle, and OB drawn from the centre, the angle OBD is equal to OBF ; 
also CBH is equal to BLH and BHL together, and OBH is, by construe-

tion, equal to CLU ; therefore ODD or OBF is equal to BHL. But since 
CH is equal to CL, angle CHL is equal to CLH or OBH ; therefore ABH 
is obviously equal to AIIB, and AH to AB ; hence CA, AB are together 
equal to the given sum of the sides. The construction is hence obvious. 
When the circle described on OH cuts CL in two points ; two triangles 
may be constructed answering the conditions ; and if the circle touch CL 
the two triangles will unite in one, and if the circle does nnt meet CL, then 
no triangle can be constructed with the data.

If from the point in which a normal to an ellipse meets the axis major a 
straight line be drawn at right angles to the normal, the part of either of the 
focal distances intercepted between this line and the curve will be an 
harmonic mean between the focal distances. 

[First Solution. Dr, Burns, Rochester; and Mr, Thomas Weddle, 

Newcastle.] 
Let the normal FN at the point P meet the major axis AB in N, and let 
QNQ' be drawn perpendicular to FN meeting the focal distances FP, F'P 
in Q and Q'; also let the tangent at P meet AB produced in M. 

Then since FP, F'P make equal angles with the tangent, and NPM, 
PNQ' are right angles, QPN is equal to Q'PN, QN to NQ', and PQ to PQ'. 
Moreover, the lines PM, QN being parallel, 

FP : FQ :: PM : QN :: PM : NQ' ; 
and the triangles PF'M, NF'Q' being similar, 

PM : NQ' :: PF' : F'Q'. Hence 
FP : FQ :: F'P : F'Q' ; 
or since PQ = PQ'^ 

FP : FP :: F'P — PQ : PQ — FP. 

That is, PQ or PQ' is a harmonic mean between F'P and FP.

LXXXVI. % Amicus. 



In every tetrahedron, the sum of the squares of the six edges is equal to 
four times the sum of the squares of the lines which join the middles of the 
opposite edges. 

[FiEST Solution. Mr, J. W, Elliott, Greatham,'] 

Let ABCD be the tetrahedron, and G, H, K, L, M, N, the middle points 
of the edges AC, AB, BD, BC, DA, DC respectively. 

Then, since the line drawn to bisect two sides of a triangle is parallel to 
the third side, and equal to half of it, we have 

BC = 2KN = 2HG, DC = 2MG = 2LK, 

AC = 2HL = 2MN, DA = 2KH = 2GN, 

AB = 2LG = 2KM, BD = 2HM = 2LN. 

Now since KN and HG are parallel to BC they are parallel to one another 
(Euc, xi, 9) ; and each being the half of BC, they are also equal to one 
another. Wherefore KNGH is a parallelogram, the diagonals of which are 
GK, HN ; and hence by a known property, 

2GK^ + 2HN^ = 4HK' + 4KN^ = AD'* + BC^ 

Taking in the same way the parallelograms MNHL and MKGL, and 
writing the entire results, we have 

2GK'* + 2HN2 = AD' + BC«, 

2HISP + 2ML' = BD' + KC\ 

2ML' + 2GK' = AB' + DC. 
These being added give the result stated in the theorem. 
Corollary. The three diagonals GK, HN, LM meet in one point O. 
This is easily proved to be the centre of gravity of the tetrahedron.

LXXXVII. Mr, Thomas JTeddle. 

An ellipse can be divided geometrically into n equal sectors when n is such,that 

a regular polygon^of n sides can be described geometrically ; required proof. 

[Solution. Mr. Q. W. HeamJ] 

Let it be required to divide a given ellipse into n equal sectors by lines 

drawn from its centre, when n is such that a regular polygon of n sides can 

be described. 

Let a and h be the semi-axes of the ellipse, and suppose a circle with 

-1 &amp; 
radius h described whose plane is inclined at an angle cos — to the plane 

of the ellipse, and so that the axis minor of the ellipse may be parallel to the 
plane of the circle, and the line joining the centres of the circle and ellipse 
at right angles to the plane of the former. Then the circle will be the 
orthographic projection of the ellipse, and any area in the plane of the ellipse 
projected on the plane of the circle will have to its projection the ratio h : a. 

Hence if the ellipse be divided into equal sectors then* projections will be 
equal sectors of the circle, and conversely, if a regular polygon be described 



MATHEMATICAL EXEBCISES. 227 

in the circle, and lines drawn from its angular points perpendicular to the 
plane of circle, these lines will intersect the ellipse in points which being 
joined with its centre will divide it into equal sectors. 

We may now conceive the circle and ellipse to be concentric, and a 
diameter of the circle consequently coincident with the axis minor of the 
ellipse, then, as in Descriptive Geometry, the sides of the orthographic 
cylinder will ultimately become straight lines parallel to the axis major of 
the ellipse. 

Hence the following construction : — 

On the axis minor of the ellipse describe a circle. Inscribe in it a regular 
polygon of n sides, and through its angular points draw lines parallel to the 
axis major of the ellipse, and join their points of intersection with the ellipse, 
and the centre, the ellipse will thus be divided into n equal sectors. 

From similar considerations, if a circle be described on the major axis, and 
lines be drawn parallel to the minor axis through the angular points of the 
inscribed polygon, the same thing may be effected. 

Also, since we may choose the first angle of the polygon inscribed in the 
circle arbitrarily, it follows not only that the ellipse may be geometrically 
divided into n equal secfors, but this in an infinite variety of ways. 

Also that an ellipse may (in an infinite number of ways) be divided into 
sectoreal areas which shall have given ratios, provided a circle can be so 
divided.

I am not aware that the problem, '^ To find the greatest inscribed ellipse 
to a given quadrilateral figure," has hitherto engaged the attention of any 
writer, though it leads to a discussion even more interesting than the other. 
Its investigation, it will be seen, results in a quadratic equation, one root 
of which gives the greatest inscribed ellipse, the other, an imaginary area. 
I have endeavoured to account for the origin of this imaginary expression.

Scholium I. The expression (21), as we have seen, does not fulfil the 
conditions of the problem, inasmuch as it gives an imaginary expression for 
the area of the inscribed ellipse. It evidently belongs to the hyperbola of 
which ac — ft* is a maximum, and to which the sides of the given quadrila- 
teral are tangential. We might indeed have anticipated such a result from 
the fundamental equation (5) which represents central curves in general. 

We may now advert for a little to the line (19) which is the locus of the 
centres of all conic sections which touch the sides or the sides produced, of 
the given quadrilateral, PQRS. 

The coordinates of the middle points of the diagonals PR, QS, are 
evidently

and as these satisfy (19), it follows that 

All conic sections that touch the sides or the sides produced of a 
given quadrilateraly have their centres situated on the straight line 
7ohich bisects the diagonals of tlie quadrilateraL 

This property has been noticed in respect of the ellipse. The hyperbolas 
obviously touch QR, RS ; and PQ, PS produced.

NOTE ON PRATT'S DEMONSTRATION OF THE PARALLELO- 
GRAM OF FORCES. 
[Mr, J, F, Heather, A,M., Royal Military Academy.] 
Pratt in his " Mechanical Philosophy" attempts to give a completely 
analytical proof of the principle of the parallelogram of forces, and founds 
it upon an assumption, which he expresses in the following terms : — 
" That a force can produce no effect in a direction perpendicular to its own 
direction." Now I think I shall be able to show that this expression must 
either have no meaning at all, or that it must be taken in the same sense as 
though we should say, " a force can produce no effect in any direction what- 
ever except its own." For if it be meant to say that a force cannot be 
resolved into two others, the direction of one of which shall be at right angles 
to its own direction, the assertion would be manifestly incorrect ; but, if it 
be only meant to say that a number of forces, having their directions all 
lying along two straight lines at right angles to each other, must have the 
algebraic sum of the forces acting along the one of these lines, and also the 
sum of those acting along the other separately equal to zero, in order that 
the whole may be in equilibrium, then the proposition is equally true of a 
number of forces having their directions all lying along any two straight 
lines, inclined at any angle whatever. This is evident from the equation 
R' = P* + Q* + 2PQ cos a : and also follows immediately, from the con- 
sideration, that any two forces acting upon a point, in directions not along 
the same straight lines must have a finite resultant ; for if the forces acting 
along any two lines are not separately in equilibrium, they all resolve into 
two forces, which not acting in opposite directions, along the same straight 
line, cannot counteract each other's effects. The values oi/{9) for the par- 
ticular values of 9 set down by Mr. Pratt, are nevertheless true, because in 
each of these particular cases, one of the two directions into which the power 
P is resolved, is in the same line with its own direction, in which case the 
component along the other direction vanishes, whatever be the angle which 
this du'ection makes with the direction of P. 



PRACTICAL METHOD OF FORMING LOGARITHMS AND 

ANTI-LOGARITHMS, INDEPENDENTLY OF 

EXTENSIVE TABLES. 

\_Profes8or Heam^ Boyal Military College,'] 

The accompanying concise tables are intended for obtaining the logarithms 
of numbers consisting of ten or less than ten digits to ten places of the 
mantissa of the logarithm. If fewer places be required the tables are of 
course equally available, and the indispensable calculation considerably 
curtailed. Again, if logs, to 20 figures were required, tables for finding 
them might easily be framed on the same principle, and they would be but 
double the extent of those here presented. 

In the " Anti-Logarithmic" set, Table I. contains the logarithms of the 
nine digits; ii. contains the logarithms of 11, 12 etc, to 19 inclusive; iiu 
contains those of 101, 102, \0Z etc, to 109 inclusive ; it. contains those of 
1001, 1002, 1003 etc, to 1009 inclusive, and so on. 

In the " Logarithmic" set. Table i. contains the arithmetical complements 
of the logarithms of 10 — 1 , 10 — 2, 10 — 3 ^/tf. to 10 — 9, or of 9, 8, 7, 
etc, to 1; II. contains the ar. comp. log. of 100 — 1, 100 — 2 etc, to 
100 — 9, or 99, 98, 97 etc, to 91 ; iii. those of 1000— 1, 1000 — 2 etc. 
to 1000 — 9, or 999, 998 etc. to 991 , and so on. 

After this brief but suflScient desciipliOTi Nf ^ VtX^ V«&gt;J»j^ ^V. tw^^-osl \^'«»^

4 taken out. The process is continued on the second remainder, etc.

the point xyz through the remote angular points, the coordinates of the * 
centre of gravity of each of the pyramids so formed become known from 
equations (5). Hence if w^w\etc,t be the weights of the pyramids, and 
x^iZi the centre of gravity of the polyhedron, we shall have {A) 

_ w{x+x'+.v"+x'" ) +w'{x+x"+x"'+x"")+eic. ' 



^ rf^ {y+y+y"+y"')+ n^'{ y+y"+y"+y"")+etc, 

^ 4(w-f-w'4- etc) 

__ w{z+z'+z"+z"')+w'{z+z"+z"'+z"")+etc. 

^ "" 4{w+w'+etc,) 



.(6). 



8. Scholium. ^^Slnce each of the triangular pyramids of which the poly- 
hedron is composed is supposed to be homogeneous, the weights w, w', etc. 
are proportional to the volumes of the pyramids ; and these may readily be 
expressed in terms of the coordinates xyz, x'y'z', etc. Hence equations 
(6) determine the coordinates of the centre of gravity of the polyhedron as 
functions of the coordinates of its angular points. It is evident also that (2) 
and (4) determine the coordinates of the centre of gravity of the perimeter 
and surface of a polygon, as functions of the coordinates of its angular 
points; since the quantities w, w', ^^tf. , are proportional to the lengths of 
the sides of the polygon in the former case ; and to the areas of the triangles 
of which it is composed in the latter ; and both of these are easily expressed 
in terms of those coordinates. 

By comparing equation (B) with (4) it will readily appear that the centre 
of gravity of a system of equal weights placed at the angular points of a 
polygon is, in general, different from the centre of gravity of its surface. 
From a remark in Lardner's Algebraic Geometry, No. 70, it would seem 
that the author was under the impression that these points are always 
identical. It is evident, however, that this is not generally the case, except 
when the figure is a triangle. 

The above propositions occurred to me some years ago, when I was en- 
gaged in drawing up a course of lectures on statics. I have found that 
equations (2) are not new, but the rest, as far as I know, have never been 
published. The mode of investigation seems to be as simple as could be 
desired; and, as the formulas are of some practical importance, I should 
think that they might be interesting to the junior readers of the Mathe- 
matician. 

JUanchester, Dec. 4th, 1846.

ON THE DETERMINATION OF THE NATURE OF SURFACES 
OF THE SECOND DEGREE. 
[James Cockle, Esq,, M,A,, Barrister^at^Law,']

^ Now, (the coordinates being x, t/, and z,) Z\ and Z'2 are linear, and Z" 
is a quadratic function of ;2; ; so that (4) is a quadratic equation in z. 

The equations (1) and (4) serve to determine completely the nature of the 
surface represented by our introductory equation. But my object in this 
paper is, not to discuss those equations, but to illustrate by an example, (1) 
a very simple way of determining ai and a' (which have each only one value) 
and Si and s' (which may each have one, two, or an infinite number of 
values), and (2) the inferepces to be drawn when those quantities are deter- 
mined. In the following process the reader will recognize a method which 
I gave at p. 114 of vol. i. of the Mathematician ; the example itself is a 
particular case of my equation (a) at p. 344 of vol. xly. of the Mechanics' 
Magazine. 

Example. — What is the surface represented by the equation 

x^+x—y^+(2z+l)y+z^+Z'--4: = ? 

Add and subtract i ; then we have 

{a,+^y-y^+(2z+l)y+z'&gt;+z-4l = 

Add and subtract J {2z + 1)S the square of half the coeflficient of y ; then 
this last equation becomes 

(^+iy - {y-^-ir ^2z'+2z^A z= (5) 

Let 2z^+2z — 4 =0; therefore z ^=^1 or — 2, and (5) becomes, on de- 
composing the first two terms into factors, 

(af''-^+z+l)(x+y^z) =0. 

Hence, since this last equation indicates a system of two straight lines, we 
see that the surface in question is one which admits of rectilinear intersection 



26S ON THE EQUATIONS TO SURFACES OF THE SECOND ORDER 

xvith two and only two planes parallel to a coordinate plane. The surface 
represented by the given equation is, consequently, the hyperboloid of one 
sheet. 

The hyperboloid of one sheet has rectilinear intersection either with two 
planes parallel to a coordinate plane, or with «o«^— the hyperbolic para- 
boloid with either one such plane or with none. This case must be dis- 
tinguished from that in which the two planes mentioned in the first case 
coincide — when this last obtains tlie given surface is a cone and the above 
equation in z would have equal roots ; while in the case of the hyperbolic 
paraboloid it has essentially but one root. In the term' " plane parallel to 
a coordinate plane'' I include the coordinate plane itself; t.^., Uie case in 
which z is zero. 

The discussion in the last paragraph is not to be considered as complete. 
For instance we have not pointed out (in connection with the method of this 
paper) to Tioro many of the coordinate planes the rectilinear sections may 
be parallel in the case of each particular surface. On commencing tl» 
operations in the above example with z instead of x^ (x being isolated, and 
determined separately), it will be seen that there are in that case two such 
planes ; and there are only two, for there are no real rectilinear intersec- 
tions parallel to the plane of x z — as will be seen on isolating y. 

I might now proceed to discuss the cases in which ai, a', «i, «', z^ are (all 
or some) unreal. For instance, when the planes represented by V = and 
W = are real, and the values of z are unreal and unequal, the surface is 
the hyperboloid of two sheets. 

The subject of this paper is connected with that of a question which I 
have proposed for solution in the Lady's and Gentleman's Diary for 
1847. As the correspondents of that valuable work will probably give 
results which (in substance at least) will be equivalent to the development 
of (4), I shall say nothing upon that point, but merely suggest the desirable- 
ness of obtaining, if possible, some convenient working formulse for the dis- 
crimination of the nature of surfiices of the second degree. Nor shall I do 
more than allude to the fact that in certain cases our process becomes 
inapplicable, for instance when the squares disappear.

[Besides the solutions of Messrs. Heam and Weddle of Exercise 87, given in 
our last number (Mr. Weddle's is given at page 182), a correct and elegant 
solution was also received from Mr. R. Harley, fforthwich, Cheshire.] 

LXXXVIIL Mr. FenwicJc. 
The equation in general of a surface of the second order when referred to 
any three coordinate planes, involves nine independent coefficients; if, 
however, the planes of reference be tangential to tiie surface, three of the 
nine coefficients will be functions of the remaining six. It is required to 
express the equation of the surface in terms of the six coefficients. 
[SoLUTioK. Mr. O. W, Heam,'] 
The equation to a surface of the second order touched by three given ^ 
planes w = 0, t? = 0, w = 0, is 

AV + BV + CW — 2ABuv — 2 ACww — BCvro 

=z[aaf -\-by + CZ + ]]^. "*■ 

For suppose in this equation one of the three u, v, w, as for example, 
u is zero, the equation reduces to 

(By— Cw?)*= {aa! + b^ + cz + l]\ 
or Bv — Cw= ^lax + biz + cz + l], 

which together with the equation w = 0, will either represent two straight 
lines, or a point, the former when A, B, C are real, the latter when they 
are of the form aV — 1. 

In the former case, the surface is the hyperboloid of one sheet (including 
the cone), in the latter, any of the other surfaces of the second order. 

That the plane w = touches the surface is manifest from the con- 
sideration that when the equation w = is combined with the equation of 
the surface, it gives either a point or two straight lines. 

It seems necessary to say a few words in addition as to the case of 
the cone. 

We must here consider A, B, C infinitely great compared with a, b, c 
or 1, so that the equation becomes 

A^u" 4- BV + CW — IkBuv — 2kCuw—1BGvw = 0, 
which being homogeneous in w, t) and w, is evidently the equation to a cone 
whose vertex is determined by « = 0, «? = 0, w = 0. Also w, Vy and ro 
are tangential planes, for putting w = 0, the equation is 

Bt&gt; — Cw = ; 
so that w = 0, and Bo — Qw = 0, must represent a straight line, that is, 
uz=:.0 intersects the cone in one straight line, and therefore touches it. 

It must be observed, that in the foregoing I have not restricted myself to 
the condition that the planes of reference shall be the tangent plafles. 
Introducing this condition we have for the equation 

Aar» + B^ + Cz^ — 2AB;ry — 2ACxz — 2BCt/z 
= {aa! + hi/ + cz + l}% 
where A, B, Cy ayb, c are the six arbitrary constants.
