XC. Mr, Fenwich. 
Investigate the locus of the centres of surfaces of the second order 
inscribed in a given parallelopiped. 

[Solution. Mr, Thomas Weddle ; and similarly by Mr. G, W. HeamJ] 

The locus of the middle points of straight lipes joining points in two 
parallel planes is a plane parallel to and equidistant from them. Now if a 
surface of the second order touch two parallel planes, the straight line join- 
ing the points of contact will pass through the centre and be bisected in it. 
Hence, if surfaces of the second order touch two given parallel planes, the 
locus of their centres is a plane parallel to and equidistant from them. 
Applying this property to surfaces of the second order inscribed in a given 
parallelopiped, we easily see that their centres will be at the same time in 
three planes passing through the intersections of the diagonals of the 
parallelopiped and parallel to its faces; that is, the point in which the 
diagonals of the parallelopiped intersect is the centre of all the surfaces of 
the second order inscribed in it. Hence the locus spoken of in this exercise 
degenerates into a point. 



266 SOLUTIONS OF MAIHEHAtlCAL EXSBCI8B8. 

It easily follows, that if four planes A, B, C, D be respectively parallel 
to other four K, L, M, N, it will, in generaly be impossible to describe a 
surface of the second order to touch all these eight planes * for, if this be 
possible, the intersections of the diagonals of the two parallelopipeds whose 
faces are A, B, C, K, L, M, and B, C, D, L, M, N respectively, must 
coincide.

DAF = ^, and AF = AE. Again angle D is the complement of angle 
E, and angle AFD is the complement of angle AFE ; hence angle AFD 
= angle D, and AF = AD.

XCVIII. Pen-and-ink, Charlton. 

Two points and a straight line, not all in the same plane, being given, it 
is required to draw two lines from the given points to meet in the given line : 

(1) That shall make equal angles with the line ; 

(2) That shall make the greatest angle with each other. 

[SoLUTioy. Mr, Thomas Weddle,'] 
r,Let A and B be the two given points (the student will readily construct 
the fig.), CD the 'given line, and first let P be a point in CD such that AP 
and BP make equal angles with the opposite portions of CD. From A 
and B draw AC and BD perpendicular to CD; let the plane BDC, be 
turned round CD until BD come into the plane ACD, and let the rotation 
be such that AC and BD may lie on opposite sides of CD. It is plain that 
BP will inf. this revolution describe a right cone ; hence AP and B'P (the 
new position of BP) will make equal angles with the opposite parts of CD, 

. N N 



278 SOLTFTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXEBCISES. 

and consequently being on different sides of CD, they must be in the same 
straight line ; also B'D is evidently perpendicular to CD. Hence the fol- 
lowing construction. 

From the given points A and B draw AC and BD perpendicular to the 
given line CD ; in the plane ACD, and on the side of CD opposite to AC, 
draw B'D perpendicular to CD and = BD ; join AB' intersecting CD in 
P, join also BP ; AP and BP will make equal angles with the opposite 
segments of CD. 

If the lines AP and BP are to make equal angles with the same part of 
CD, it might similarly be shown that the construction will be the very same 
except that B'D must be drawn on the same side of CD as AC. 

Next let F be such a point in CD that the angle AP'B may be a maxi- 
mum. From A and B draw AC and BC perpendicular to CD, and bisect 
CD in E. Let AE = a, BE = h, CE = DE = c, EP' = ar, and

Solutions were also received from Messrs. Ambler, Godfray, and Mortimer. 
(Proposed in Vol. I., No. 5, p. 272.) 

In laying down fifteen points upon a plane, they may be so placed that 
sets of three points ranging exactly in a line, shall exist in twenty-six 
different du-ections. Required proof. 

[Solution. By the Proposer,'] 

An extreme limit may immediately be assigned to the question abstractedly 
merely by the mode in which the points must necessarily be associated. 
Each line or range exhibits a triad of points, and amongst all these triads it 
is evident that any two points cannot appear together more than once, because 
the repetition of a pair of points would imply a repetition of the same line. 
The complete number of triads that can be thus collected out of 15 things 
will comprise the whole of the 105 pairs,* and as each triad involves three 
distinct pairs, the total number of such triads is 35. We therefore conclude, 
without reference to geometrical conditions or limitations, that the greatest 
possible number of ranges cannot exceed the number 35. 

It will appear that a general discussion of the problem with the points in 
space can embrace no more possible ranges than in piano. This is obvious 

• See the Ladj's and Gentleman's Diary for 1847, page 62, solution to question xii. (1760.) 



SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL £X£KCISES. 279 

by considering the orthographic projection of the figure upon any plane not 
coincident with planes normal to the several lines. We shall therefore treat 
the question under its more general aspect. 

Three unequal spheres 8182^^ being taken ad libitum, both as to magnitude 
and position, it is well known that cones enveloping each pair have their 
three vertices ranging in a right line. Let the point which is the vertex of 
the cone enveloping 8182 be denoted by (I, 2); the vertex of the cone en- 
veloping *2^3 by (2, 3), etc. Then the three vertices (1,2), (2, 3), (1, 3) 
range in a right line, which may be conveniently designated (I, 2, 3). 
Instead of three let us now take six spheres ^1^2*3 818586 ; then every pair of 
spheres determining a point, there will be 15 points as in the question. Also 
the points resulting from every triad of spheres constituting a range, the 
number of ranges will be 20, being the number of different triads out of six 
things. We shall therefore have 20 ranges amongst the fifteen points with 

the six spheres taken ad libitum . . , , , , , , • • • (a). 

It remains to determine how many more ranges can possibly be deter- 
mined by varying the magnitude and position of one or more of the six spheres. 
The triads of points furnished by the 20 ranges (a) are distinguished by the 
repetition of the numerals. Thus (2, 3), (3, 5), in which 3 is repeated, are 
situated in the line (2, 3, 5) and can therefore only be associated with the 
point (2, 5) and form one of the twenty ranges already determined. Con- 
sequently in putting down new triads of points we must in each of them 
employ all the six spheres, as no one can be repeated. It is easy therefore 
to write all the additional triads as follows : — 

(16, 23, 45), (16, 24, 35), (16, 25, 34) (A) 

(26, 13, 45), (26, 14, 35), (26, 15, 34) (B) 

(36, 12, 45), (36, 14, 25), (36, 15, 24) (C) 

(46, 12, 35), (46, 13, 25), (46, 15, 23) (D) 

{56, 12, 34), {56, 13, 24), (56, 14, 23) (E) 

These 15 triads make up the complete set of 35 triads before mentioned, 
and any one of them may separately be converted into a new range by vary- 
ing the magnitude and position of one of the spheres. We must now ascer- 
tain how many of them can be made to range simultaneously, consistently 
with the geometrical relations of the figure. 

First take the three triads in (A) and let us direct our attention for a 
a moment to the four spheres 828384,85. As they comprise four different 
triads the six resulting points will have four ranges and will ^evidently be 
the six corners to a complete quadrilateral whose prolonged sides are the 
four lines (2, 3, 4,), (2, 3, 5), (2, 4, 5,) (3, 4, 5). The three diagonals of 
this quadrilateral are the lines joining the pairs of points (23, 45), (24, 35), 
(25, 34). According to (A) the point (1, 6) is required to range in each of 
these three diagonals, which is impossible, as the three diagonals of a 
quadrilateral cannot intersect in a common point. The point (1,6) may 
however be placed on the intersection of any two of the three diagonals 
without ranging in the third. We must therefore reject at least one of the 
triads from each of the sets (A), (B), (C), (D), (E). Let the first triad be 
cancelled in each of the sets (A), (B, (C), and the ranges suggested by these 
sets will be 



'i 



(16, 24, 35), (16, 25, 34)- 

(26, 14, 35), (26, 15, 34) J. (fi) 

(36, 14, 25), (36, 15,24), 



280 SOLUTIOKS OF MATHEMATICAL EXEIICISE8. 

It will be necessary to show that the points (1,6), (2, 6), (3, 6), thus 
determined by the first five spheres, are consistent with the previously 
assigned ranges (12, 16. 2()), (13, 16, 36), (23, 26, 36). Let two lines be 
drawn, the one joining the centre of Si with the point (3, 5) , and the other 
joining the centre of Si with the point (3, 4). These lines being both 
situated in the plane passing through the centres of Sa^iS^, will intersect. 
Let the point in which they intersect be taken for the centre of another 
sphere Sj, and let its magnitude be such that the point (3, 7) may be situated 
in the line (3, 4, 5). Then since the point (3, 7) ranges with (3, 4), (4, 7) 
and also with (3, 4), (3, 5) it is evident that the point (4, 7) must coincide 
with (3, 5). Similarly the point (.'), 7) coincides with (3, 4). Therefore 
the first and second lines of {ft) may be written 

(16, 24, 47), (16, 25, 57), 
(26, 14,47), (26, 15,57). 

Hence the points (I, 6), (2, 6) determined by (ft) will respectively coincide 
with (2, 7), (1,7), and must therefore range in the same line with the point 
(1,2). The other two ranges may be similarly exhibited, and thus the 
points (1, 6), (2, 6), (3, 6) which result from the ranges in (/§), are such as 
to satisfy the previously implied conditions. 

The six triads contained in (D), (E) will now, every one of them, be 
individually excluded. For example take the first in (D) and it will appear 
that (3, 5) is required to range with (12, 46). But in (ft) it already ranges 
in (16, 24), (26, 14), two of the diagonals of the quadrilateral (1, 2, 4, 6), 
and it therefore cannot range in the third. The other triads in (D), (E) are 
all similarly inadmissible. 

We have therefore, in all, 26 possible ranges amongst the fifteen points. 

For the construction of the diagram the intervention of the spheres will 
not be required. Any three points (I, 2), (1,3), (2, 3) being taken in a 
line, let three lines be drawn respectively through them, those from (1, 2), 
(1,3) intersecting in (1,4), those from (1,2), (2, 3), in (2, 4), and those 
from (1,3), (2, 3) in (3, 4). In like manner, through the same three points 
as before, let any other three lines be drawn intersecting each other in the 
•points (1, 5), (2, 5), (3, 5). By {ft) the points (1, 6), (2, 6), (3, 6) are now 
determined by the respective intersections of the diagonals therein indicated. 
The three remaining points are 
(4, 5) the mutual intersection of (14, 15), (24, 25), (34, 35), 
(4,6) „ (14, 16), (24, 26), (34, 36), 

(5, 6) „ (15, 16), (25, 26), (35, 36), (45, 46), 

and the three remaining ranges are (12, 16, 26), (13, 16, 36), (23, 26, 36). 

Since every four spheres determine a complete quadrilateral the entire 
figure will contain fifteen complete quadrilaterals, being as many as there are 
points. These quadrilaterals will have amongst them twenty different sides 
exhibiting the twenty ranges according to (a), and the other six ranges in 
{ft) will be diagonal ranges involving additional lines.

PAET I. 

Properties of the complete inscribed and circumscribed qtiadrilaieralSf 
to a St/stem of curves of the second order. 

' My chief object in view in the first part of the subsequent discussion is, 
to establish a connected system of lines and points, by which every property 
of the plane quadrilateral may readily be deduced. How far I have 
succeeded in this respect, must be left to others to determine. At any rate 
I expect that I have added to the many properties already known of the 
quadrilateral and the series of conic sections by which it is necessarily 
circumscribed. 

The second part relates to properties of the centres of gravity of a 
quadrilateral and the triangles of which it is composed. Though many of 
these are known, their investigation, I trust, will be found interesting to 
some of our readers. When I say knoron^ I mean they are known to those 
mathematicians who read mathematical works published on the continent, 
for I am not aware that they are given in any English work. ^

The lines KM, NL, therefore, intersect in the line OQ. Hence the 
following theorems: — 

{x) If we join the centres of gravity of the four triangles formed hy 
the two diagonals of a quadrilateral^ and which have for their common 
vertex^ th^e point of intersection of tlie diagonals^ we shall form a . 
parallelogram^ whose sides are parallel to the diagonals of the 
quadrilateral. 

ly) The sides of this parallelogram contain the centres of gramty 
of the triangles which two and two compose the quadrilateral^ and its 
diagonals intersect on the line which passes through the centre of 
gravity and point of intersection of the diagonals, of the quadrilateral. 

{z) The surface of this parallelogram is two ninths of the surface 
of the quadrilcUeraL 

By means of the centres of gravity of the eight triangles which are formed 
by the diagonals of the original quadrilateral, we can thus form a paral- 
lelogram and a new inscribed quadrilateral ; and by continuing the process 
we get a series of parallelograms and inscribed quadrilaterals, the areas of 
the latter being denoted by the series 

"sT ^' "sT ^' 729 ^' ^'^•' 
where A is the area of the original quadrilateral* The diagonals of all the 
quadrilaterals and parallelograms intersect on the line which passes through 
the centre of gravity and intersection of the diagonals, of the original 
quadrilateral. 

Moreover, if we join the middle points of the adjacent sides of the original 
quadrilateral, a parallelogram will be formed, having its sides parallel to the 
diagonals of the quadrilateral. And if we join the points in which the sides 
of this parallelogram cut the diagonals of the original quadrilateral, another 
quadrilateral will be formed similar to the original one ; and so on. These 
parallelograms and quadrilaterals have also a remarkable relation to each 
other. But we need do no more than direct the student's attention to them. 
We may remark, however, that all the diagonals of these parallelograms 
and quadrilaterals intersect, as in the former series, iu the line OQ.

We may take this opportunity to state, in reply to several inquiries^ 
that many of our papers in the " Mathematician'* may be considered as 
continuations of the " Geometry of Coordinates." When the first part 
appeared, the publication of the Mathematician was not contemplated. 
- Our little treatise on this subject has been translated into German by 
Dr. Wiegand, Professor of Mathematics and Natural Philosophy, at Halle. 
The following is a translation of Dr. Wiegand's preface. 



296 



ALGEBRAIC DEM0NSTRATI0E7, ETC 



•* When I first saw the Elementary Propositions, &amp;c., by W. Rutherford 
and S. Fenwick, of the Royal Military Academy at Woolwich, I was so 
much struck with the easy and intelligible treatment of the theorems in this 
branch of geometry, that they seemed to me well deserving to be more 
generally made known. My expectations have not been disappointed, for 
the translation of the first number has gone through two editions in a very 
short time. 

** Though the mathematical literature of Germany is by no means poor in 
comprehensive and excellent works in this particular branch, and indeed we 
may confidently say, that the works of our ** Plucker" are scarcely equalled 
by all the foreign works put together, yet the important results at which he 
has arrived are still very far from being known to the mathematical world, 
in proportion to their merit. 

" My object will be fully answered if, by publishing the following papers, 
I contribute in some measure to the circulation of new views in analytical 
investigation. Haying established a direct correspondence with the 
authors, the first of whom is already well known to mathematicians in 
Germany, I shall i)e able to publish the subsequent numbers immediately 
after their appearance in England."

ON THE DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL 

VOLUMES. 

IMr. Thomas Weddle, NewcasUe-upon-TyneJ] 

The object of the following paper is the discussion of the problem, 

To divide a given ellipsoid into n equal volumes^ 

and as I am not aware that this has been attempted before, I trust the 
subject will be found to possess some degree of interest. 

I shall first dispose of two or three particular solutions which it will be 
advantageous to consider independently of the general method. One of 
these solutions is as follows : 

I. Draw any diameter A'OA of the ellipsoid, and a diametral plane 
conjugate to it ; the section of the ellipsoid by this plane will be an ellipse ; 
by (D) of my paper entitled " Properties of the Ellipse," Mathematician, 
Vol. II. p. 181, divide this ellipse into n equal sectors P1OP2, P2OP3. . . . 
Pn-iOPn, PnOP, ; through A'A and each of the lines 0P„ OP2. . . .OPn, 
let a plane be made to pass, then will the ellipsoid be divided into n equal 
wedges by these n planes. 

This does not require a formal demonstration ; its truth is evident from 
the consideration that each plane section of the ellipsoid parallel to that of 
the diametral plane is divided into n equal sectors by its intersection with 
the n planes. 

Moreover the preceding construction is not confined to an entire ellipsoid ; 
it will apply to any finite portion (cut oflf by parallel planes) of an ellipsoid, 
an hyperboloid of one or two sheets, an elliptic paraboloid, a cone or an 
elliptic cylinder. We may here proceed in the following manner. 

r. Since the volume cut off is finite, the sections made by the parallel 
planes are necessarily ellipses ; divide either of these into n equal sectorB 
PiOPg, . . .PnOPi, (as above); find the diameter conjugate to the parallel 



ox THE DIVISION OP AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQTTAL VOLXTMES. 303 

planes (which may be done by joining the centres 0', 0, of the sections), 
and through O'O and the lines OPi, 0P2,,..0P» let planes pass, these 
will divide the solid into n equal wedges. 

It will not fail to be observed that the constructions I and V will still 
apply when . the problem is to divide the solid into portions having given 
ratios to each other, provided the ellipse be divided into sectors having these 
ratios instead of into equal parts. These processes also hold with respect to 
portions of the solids cut off by planes passing through A'A (or O'O), the 
figure to be divided into equal parts or into parts having given ratios, being 
now a sector of an ellipse. (See (T) Mathematician, Vol II. p. 186.) 

Of course none of the preceding operations will be geometrical unless 
the division of the ellipse (or elliptic sector, as the case may be) can be 
performed geometrically. A similar remark is also applicable to some of 
the succeeding solutions. 

Before proceeding to the next method, it will be necessary to premise the 
following lemmas (o) and (/3) . 

(a). In A' A any diameter of an ellipsoid, take any point P, through 
which let a plane be drawn parallel to the diametral plane conjugate to A'A : 
with the centre O as vertex and curve of section as director, let a cone be 
described ; this conical surface will cut off a solid sector which is to the 
whole ellipsoid as AP is to A'A. 

Take any two diameters B'B, C'C (which will be in the diametral plane) 
forming with A'A a system of conjugate diameters. Denote AO, BO, CO,

to each other), and it -is easy to see that the volume of that part of the cone

It also easily follows from (a), that, 

{p). If through two points, P and Q, in a diameter A'A, planes be drawn 
and conical surfaces be described as in (a), the portion of the ellipsoid in- 
cluded between these two surfaces is to the whole ellipsoid as PQ : A'A. 

We are now prepared for our second solution. 

II. Take any diameter A'A of the ellipsoid, divide it into n equal parts, 
and through the n — I points of division let planes be drawn parallel to the 
diametral plane conjugate to A'A ; with the centre as common vertex and 
the curves in which these planes cut the ellipsoid as directors, letw— 1 
conical surfaces be described ; then will these surfaces divide the ellipsoid 



304 OV TUE DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL VOLUMES. 

into n equal parts. If A' A, instead of being divided into equal parts, be 
divided into parts having any ratios to each other, the ellipsoid will be divided 
into volumes having these ratios. 

This construction, the truth of which is evident from (a, /3), is very simple 
and general ; it requires, however, the description of cones of the second 
order, so that it is not strictly geometrical. 

The preceding solution will also apply to a portion of an ellipsoid cut off 
by parallel planes, as follows : 

ir. Divide the line joining the centres of the sections into n equal parts 
or into n parts having the given ratios, and draw planes parallel to those of 
the sections and describe cones as in II. 

A little consideration will make it evident that if a wedge be cut off from 
an ellipsoid by two planes passing through A'A, we may vary (a) by 
saying, — tlie corrresponding portion of the solid sector is to the wedge in 
the same ratio as before, namely, AP to A'A ; hence also, instead of (/3) we 
shall liave, the portion of the wedge included between the two cones is to 
the whole wedge as PQ is to A'A. From ihose considerations it appears 
that the process II will effect the division of a wedge cut off by planes 
passing through the diameter employed, into equal parts, (or parts having 
given ratios), and hence, when w is a composite number = jE?g', another 
method of dividing an ellipsoid into n equal volumes may be got by com- 
bining I and II in the following manner. 

III. Divide the ellipsoid into p equal parts by I, and into q equal parts 
by II, using the same diameter A'A in both operations ; then, by the 
mutual intersections of the p planes, and q — 1 cones, the ellipsoid will be 
divided into jt?^' equal volumes. 

In the paper alluded to in I, I have virtually shown that the division of 
an ellipse into equal sectors may be made to depend on that of the circum- 
ference of a circle into equal arcs ; I shall now endeavour to establish the 
analogous property in space, namely, that the division of an ellipsoid into 
equal volumes may be effected when the surface of a sphere can be divided 
into as many equal surfaces. 

a?' y^ z^ x^ tP z^ 

Let — -|- ^ -J — 0=1, and —^ + ^ + — = 1 , be the equations to two 

ellipsoids, (either to the same or different axes) ; (a?i, ^/u ^1) a point on the 
surface of the former, and (5^3, ^2? ^2) a point on that of the latter ; also let

the points {xi, ^i, Zi) and (;r2, y^^ Zn)^ so related, are called corresponding 
points ; also if two points move on the surfaces so as to be always corres- 
ponding, they will trace out corresponding curves. 

From the three preceding equations we see that if the coordinates of one 
of two corresponding points be given, those of the other will be got by 
simply finding fourth proportionals, and it will be useful to observe that if 
one of the points be denoted by (a/, hm, cn\ where /, 7W, n are subject to 
the condition £^ -\- m^ -\- n^ :=. 1, the other will be denoted by (c?/, em^fn). 

If one of two corresponding curves, that on the surface of the first ellip. 
sold for instance, be a plane curve, the other will be plane also, for if the 
phne Ldx -)- M^y +N/';2^ = P, contain the point (a/, hm, en), the plane 



ON THB DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL- VOLUMES. 305 

Jjax 4- M&amp;y 4- N^^ = P, will contain the point {dly em,fn)^ for the con- 
dition in each case is hadl + Mbem + ^cjh = P. 

Hence if any plane section of an ellipsoid be given, the corresponding 
curve on any other ellipsoid may be found. Take three points on the given 
curve and find the corresponding points on the other ellipsoid ; the plane 
passing through these three points will intersect the ellipsoid in the curve 
required. Moreover, if the plane of one of two corresponding plane curves 
pass through the centre of the ellipsoid on which it is situated, we shall have 
P = 0, in the preceding equations, and consequently the plane of the second 
curve will pass through the centre of the other ellipsoid. Hence, as in this 
case we already know one point in the plane of the second curve, only two 
corresponding points will be required to construct it ; these two points must 
not however be diametrically opposite. 

Finally, we must establish the following theorem. 

(7). If corresponding closed curves* on two ellipsoids be the directors 
of two curves, having their vertices at the respective centres, the solid 
sectors cut off by the conical surfaces will be proportional to the ellipsoids 
themselves, f 

Let the portions of the surfaces of the ellipsoids contained within the curves 
be covered with an inunense number of corresponding points. Let P, Q, R 
be three adjacent points on one of the surfaces, and /?, q, r the corresponding 
points on the other. The plane triangles PQR, pqr will, on account of 
their extreme smallness, nearly coincide with the surfaces, and the triangular 
pyramids OPQR, opqr^ (0 and o being the centres of the ellipsoids), may 
be taken as the elements of the solid sectors whose volumes, V and t?, we 
wish to compare. Denote the points P,Q,R by (a/i, ftwi, cn^^ (a/j, ftw2, ^7^^), 
(a/3, hniz^cn^^ then the corresponding ppints /&gt;, q^ r will be denoted by 
(dli, emit frii), {dlz, emz, fn^) , {di^, em^j/nz)' Hence (Young's Anal. 
Geom., Vol. II, p. 243),

which establishes the proposition, for the ratio of the ellipsoids is the same as 
dbc : def. 

If one of the ellipsoids be a sphere, and its surface be divided by curves 
into n surfaces equal in area, and cones having these curves for directors 
and the centre for common vertex be described, then will the n solid sectors 
into which the sphere is thus divided be equal to each other. It hence 

* It must be borne in mind that these curves may be discontinuous and then the cones 
will be so too. Also, if any portion of such a curve be contained in a plane passing through 
the centre of the ellipsoid, a part of the cone, having the curve for director and its vertex at' 
the centre, will coincide with a portion of this plane. 

t I find that this theorem has been previously given (as a lemma) by Mr. Jellet in a 
paper" On the Quadrature of Surfaces of the Second Order." See the Camb. and Dublin 
Math. Journal, new series, No. I, p. 59. 



306 ON THE DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL YOLUlfSS. 

follows, with the aid of (7) , that if the curves on the ellipsoid corresponding 
to those on the sphere be found, the ellipsoid will be divided into n equal 
volumes by the description of cones having these curves for directors and the 
centre for common vertex. Now, if the curves dividing the spherical sur- 
face be plane, that is, the arcs of either great or small circles, we know from 
what has been previously shown that the corresponding curves on the ellip- 
soid can be described, atid the planes containing the latter curves will 
evidently either pass through the centre or not, according as great or small 
circles are employed. Moreover it is clear that in the former case, the 
dividing cones will consist of portions of planes only, and hence the con- 
struction will be effected geometrically, which however, cannot be done when 
small circles are used. 

Returning now to the problem which it is the object of this paper to 
discuss, we shall evidently have the following 

GENERAL SOLUTION. 

(A). Divide the surface of a sphere, in any manner, into n equal surfaces 
by arcs of great or small circles, find the corresponding curves on the sur- 
face of the ellipsoid, and with these curves as directors and the centre of the 
ellipsoid as common vertex, describe cones ; these will, by their mutual inter- 
sections, divide the ellipsoid into n equal volumes. 

If small circles be excluded, the preceding process may be modified as 
follows, and it may now be termed the 

OENEBAL GEOMETRICAL SOLUTION. 

(B). Divide the surface of a sphere into n equal surfaces by the arcs of 
great circles, find the points on the ellipsoid corresponding to the angles of 
the spherical figures into which the surface of the sphere is divided, and join 
these points by straight lines in the same order as the corresponding points 
on the sphere are by the arcs of great circles ; through each of theSe straight 
lines and the centre of the ellipsoid, let planes be drawn ; then will the ellip- 
soid be divided into n equal volumes. 

N.B. Should any of the great circle arcs be a semicircle, the last 
construction, determining merely two diametrically opposite points in the 
corresponding curve on the ellipsoid, would be insufficient. In this case we 
must take a point in the semicircle and treat it as one of the angles of the 
spherical figures. 

It is evident that an ellipsoid may be divided into parts having given ratios 
to each other, by means of (A) and (B), providing the surface of the sphere 
be divided into parts having these ratios to each other instead of into equal 
parts. Also, if a closed curve, either contained entirely in one plane or 
consisting of portions of plane curves, be traced on the surface of an ellip- 
soid, the solid cut off by the conical surface having its vertex at the centre 
and the curve for director, may be divided into equal volumes, or into 
volumes having given ratios, providing the spherical figure bounded by the 
corresponding curve on a sphere, can be similarly divided. This includes as 
a particular case, an ellipsoidal wedge, that is, a portion of an ellipsoid cut 
off by two diametral planes, the spherical figure to be divided being a lune. 

All that now remains to be done in order to complete the solution of our 
problem, is merely to notice the most interesting methods of dividing a 
spherical surface into equal parts, and this I shall dispose of as briefly as 
possible. 



ON THE DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL VOLUMES. 307 

1 . The surface of a sphere may be divided into n equal parts as follows : 
Take any circle, either great or small, of the sphere, and inscribe in it a 
regular polygon of n sides, through the poles of this circle and each of the 
angles of the polygon, draw planes intersecting the sphere in n semicircles 
or meridians, then will the surface of the sphere be divided into n equal 
lunes. 

2. Take any diameter of the sphere, divide it into n equal parts, and 
through the n — I points of division draw planes perpendicular to the 
diameter; these planes will divide the surface into n equal parts. (See 
Hymers's Int. Cal., third edition, p 182.) 

3. If w be a composite number = pq, we may combine the preceding 
constructions. Divide the surface of the sphere intojt? equal parts by (1), 
and into 5- equal parts by (2), using in the latter operation the diameter 
passing through the poles of the circle employed in the first. The mutual 
intersections of the great and small circles will divide the surface of the sphere 
intojo^y equal parts. 

4. Another obvious and interesting, though very limited, method is by in- 
scribing a regular body in the sphere, and joining the angular points by arcs 
of great circles in the same order as the edges of the body. In this way we 
may divide the surface into 4, 6, 8, 12, or 20 equal and regular spherical 
figures ; and if great circle arcs be drawn from the angles of each figure to 
the near pole of its circumscribing circle, the surface of the sphere will be 
divided into 12, 24, 24, 60, or 60 equal isosceles triangles respectively, and 
the number of divisions may be doubled by bisecting the vertical angle of 
each triangle. 

5. Divide the sphere into two hemispheres, and in each describe 
|w — 1, (n even), or |(w — 1), (n odd), spherical polygons, whose areas shall 
be equal to 0, 20, 30, etc, respectively, (0 being the n^^ part of the surface 
of the sphere), and let the polygons of each hemisphere be described in such 
a manner that the m^ polygon shall contain all the preceding w — 1 poly- 
gons ; then, accounting the great circle dividing the hemispheres a polygon 
when n is even, but not when n is odd, the areas of the surfaces included 
between the polygons will be equal, and hence the surface of the sphere will 
be divided into n equal surfaces. 

The neatest mode of proceeding will be to describe regular spherical 
polygons of the same number of sides in such a way that the poles of the 
small circles circumscribing them may coincide with those of the great circle 
dividing the hemispheres. I shall now show how thfese polygons may be 
constructed. 

In the first place we may observe, that if the angles of a spherical triangle 
be given, it may be described, for the sides and angles of the polar triangle 
are respectively equal to the supplements of the angles and sides of the 
primitive triangle ; hence the polar triangle may be constructed and the 
supplements of the measures of its angles will be the sides of the primitive 
triangle. 

Let it now be required to construct a regular spherical polygon of m sides 
whose area shall be 0. Suppose AB to be a side of the polygon, and O the 
near pole of its circumscribing circle. The area of the isosceles triangle 

AOB evidently = — and &lt;{ O = — -, hence &lt;^ A+&lt;&lt; B+&lt;&lt; O — 7r= — , 
or A £= B = ^ + r~- _-^ ; the angles of the triangle AOB being now

AB = TT — &lt; O, and AO = BO = w — ^a\ a side of the regular spherical 
polygon, and the radius of its circumscribing circle being thus found, we are 
obviously enabled to describe the polygon. 

Hence we have only to take = , ...... -^ in succession, (here

/? = \n — I, when n is even, and '=-\{n — 1 ), when n is odd), and describe 
the resulting polygons in each heraiapherc, making the poles of their circum-

spheres; then, accounting as above, the great circle a polygon, or not, 
according as n is even or odd, the surliice will be divided into n equal 
surfaces. 

6. If 72 be a composite number = pq^ we may combine (I) and (5). 

Inscribe a regular (plane) polygon of jt? sides in any great circle, and 
through its poles L and M, and the angles of the polygons pass planes as 
in (I ), which will intersect the sphere in jp semicircles or meridians. Again, 
find as in (5), the radii of the small circles circumscribing those regular 
spherical polygons of jp sides that are capable of dividing the spherical 
surface into q equal parts. With L and M as poles, and these radii, 
describe small circles, and join (in order) thejt? points In which each circle 
intersects the meridians, by great circle arcs ; these arcs and the meridians 
will divide the surface of the sphere \ii\o pq equal sm*faces. 

It must be borne in mind that if n be even, the great circle first taken will 
supply the place of a small circle. 

Several other methods of dividing the surface of a sphere into equal 
surfaces might be given, but these are the most interesting ways that I have 
been able to think of. The most of them are new so far as is known to me, 
though it seems very probable that several of them will prove to be old. It 
will not fail to be observed that all the methods, except (4), may be adapted 
to divide a spherical lune into equal surfaces, and that (1), (2), and (5) may 
be modified so as to divide a spherical surface or a spherical lune into surfaces 
having given ratios. 

The methods of dividing an ellipsoid into equal volumes that may \^ got 
by combining (1), (2), and (3) with (A) or (B), have already been given 
under better forms, I, II, III. The solutions afibrded by (B), with the aid 
of (5) and (6), might also be simplified, though not very materially, and 
hence, as this paper is already much longer than I anticipated, I shall not 
dwell upon these modifications. 

I shall, in conclusion, advert to a mode of dividing the ellipsoid whicb is 
altogether different from any of the preceding. It is true, that this solution 
is never geometrical (except w = 2) as it depends on the trisection of an 
angle, while most of those previously given are, in certain cases, capable of 
being performed geometrically ; nevertheless, this method is so simple and 
elegant, that I am persuaded I ought not to omit it. 

Let A'A be any diameter of the ellipsoid, in which take the centre O and 
any point S, and through S draw a plane conjugate to A' A cutting oflT a 
segment from the ellipsoid. If OA = a, and OS = x^ it may readily be

It might also be shown that the division of a portion of an hyperboloid of 
one or two sheets, cut off by parallel planes, may be effected by the solution

generally require us to be able not only to trisect an angle, as in IV, but to 
insert two mean proportionals between given lines. Also since the portion 
of an elliptic paraboloid cut off by any plane conjugate to a given diameter is 
proportional to the square of the segment of the diameter intercepted between 
the surface and the plane, the division of an elliptic paraboloid may be 
effected ^eometriccUly. The great length, however, to which the discussion 
of the problem I proposed to myself has extended, precludes me from doing 
more than hinting at these processes.

XXXYIII. 

JFJien in any one of three given directrices a point is given y it is 
required to construct the generatrix through that point. 

Let L, Li, La be the three given directrices, and p a point in one of them,

plane /?2^,. 

Now since the planes piqup%o% pass through the point J9, they intersect in 
a straight line passing through tnat point. That line, therefore, rests upon 
the directrix L. 

Again, since the common section of the planes piqi^p^t^ is in each of the 
planes, it will cut or be parallel to all lines whatever, Li, L, situated in those 
planes ; and hence will either cut or be parallel to L^, L,. It cannot be 
parallel to both, since, by hypothesis, those lines are themselves not parallel. 
It must therefore cut one of them ; and similarly, it will cut or be parallel 
to the other. 

The condition, therefore, ofpifPi being a generatrix is fulfilled. 

Conversely y we may always consider a generatrix as the intersection of 
two planes drawn both through a given point in one directrix, and passing 
through the other two, one plane through one directrix and the othcT through 
the other.

XLI. 

When the section^plane coincides with one of the directrices it must 
also coincide with some one of the generatricesy and can only coincide 
with one. 

For as every one of the generatrices meets that directrix, there is no one 
of them which is not in a plane with that directrix. The plane, therefore, 
being conceived to revolve about the directrix, it will come into coincidence 
with every generatrix successively. Whence through any directrix, innu- 
merable planes may be drawn that shall cut the surface in a second straight 
line. 

Nor can any plane be drawn through a directrix that shall not cut the 
surface in a second straight line. 

For, one of the generatrices of the surface is situated upon every plane 
drawn through that directrix ai:^ a point in a second directrix ; and these 
include all planes drawn through tne first directrix upon which one of the 
generatrices is not placed. 

The conic section, then, in this case, is reduced to two straight lines. 
These lines will meet if the plane do not pass through the centre ; and they 
will be parallel if it do.

Moreover, as no two generatrices can be parallel, no more than one of 
thcin can be in the same given plane through a directrix. 

XLII. 

Another property is this : — 

If lines be drawn parallel to all the generating lines of either system^ 
they will constitute the ordinary cone either right or oblique :' and to 
7vhichexer system these parallels be drawn ^ the cone will be the samCy 
if drawn through tlie same point in space as its vertex.

If four planes pass through the same line^ and parallel to these four 
other planes be drawn through a fixed pointy all these planes will 
intersect in a line drawn through that point parallel to the first line. 

Let the planes A, B, C, D intersect in a line L, and through a point p

parallel to the corresponding intersections of those through L, and L2 ; and 
hence whatever other character the cone generated by these lines may have, 
it is generated, as stated in the enunciation, by lines through the given point 
parallel to the generatrices of the hyperboloid. 

Again, the eight planes through Li and Lj being parallel to those through 
/i and ^2) each to each respectively, their traces on the section-plane will be 
parallel each to each respectively ; and the figures traced upon that plane 
being composed of triangles, those figures are both similar and similarly 
situated on the section-plane. Whence the curves traced out by the sixth 
point in each system are also similar and similarly situated : and since the 
first hihhzdidriididi is a conic section the other is also a conic section ; and 
the cone itself is the ordinary one, either right or oblique.

XLIII. 

The hyperholic paraboloid. It now becomes necessary to examine what 
takes place in the (tase, which in the preceding inquiry has been formally 
concluded from our consideration : viz., when the three given lines are 
parallel to the same plane. 

The parallelepiped under these circumstances ceases to exist : for instead 
of the two planes through each line, parallel respectively to the other two 
lines, we have now only a single plane,^ which is parallel to both those 
others. Whence the system is reduced to three parallel planes. 

Bearing in mind that no condition has in the general investigation of

Now, since these parallels are generatrices of the first and second modes, they meet the

the tangents to a conic section, concentric, similar, and similarly situated, with that of the 
hyperboloid. 




314 MODSBN OEOMBTBT. 

When the plane to which the generatrices are parallel is given, two 
linear directrices only are necessary for fixing their successive positions. 
For, calling this plane the directing plane, and drawing successive planes 
parallel to it, these planes will cut the directrices each in two points, which 
determine the successive generating lines. 

The surface thus generated is called the hyperbolic paraboloid by 
Hachette, from a property which will hereafter be deduced. It had, how- 
ever, in the arts received the name of plan gauche, or twisted plane ; and 
this is still oflen employed by the French writers, though not so frequently 
as Hachette*s term. 

These two views which may be taken of the genesis of the surface, have 
led to two different definitions corresponding to them respectively : — 

( 1 ) The hyperbolic paraboloid is generated by a straight line which 
moves upon three given directrices, no two of which are in the same plane, 
but all of which are parallel to one plane : — 

(2) The twisted plane is generated by a line which moves upon two 
given straight lines not situated in the same plane, and which is always 
parallel to a given plane. 

It is scarcely necessary to remark that the first of these definitions is the 
more systematic one, taken in reference to the general investigation of the 
character of the rule-surfaces whose directrices and generatrices are recti- 
linear. The second form deduced from it, as is here done, should be 
considered as a property — not as a definition. Yet it is almost, if not 
altogether, invariably assumed by the continental writers without the 
slightest hint of such a proof as is offered above, that the two definitions 
are those of identical surfaces : — an assumption which appears to me to be 
far too remote from obviousness to justify its adoption without a distinct 
investigation. 

It may also, indeed, be remarked that a second definition of the general 
surface of the hyperboloid of one sheet is almost as frequently employed as 
that which has been described in this chapter ; and that most writers pass 
from one to the other at pleasure, without in any case taking the trouble 
(and that is not a slight trouble) to establish the identity of the two geneses, 
even by a shore of geometrical reasoning. 

XLIV. 

Under whichever aspect we view the hyperbolic paraboloid it will follow 
that it admits of two distinct modes of description, like the hyperboloid of 
one sheet. 

For, viewed as a particular case of the latter surface, the reasoning already 
employed applies without restriction to the hyperbolic paraboloid. And, 
again, viewed as the twisted plane, two given directrices and a directing 
plane being given, any two generatrices will fix a new directing plane for a 
second genesis, and these generatrices become the directrices of the second 
mode of description. '^ 

It follows, therefore, that through any point in the hyperbolic paraboloid 
two lines may always be drawn which shall lie wholly upon t^p surface ; 
that never more than two can be so drawn ; that every line of one genesis 
meets every line of the other, dividing the surface into infinitesimal twisted 
quadrilaterals ; that no line of one genesis can meet another line of the 
same genesis ; etc. 

The difference, indeed, between the hyperboloid of one sheet and the 
hyperbolic paraboloid consists wholly in those properties- which are de^ 
pendent on the parallelopiped described upon the three directrices in

MATHEMATICAT. NOTE. 

[Mr, J. F. HeaiheTy B. A., Royal Military Academy,] 

At page 66 of Walton's Collection of Mechanical Problems, I find the 
following paragraph. 

*' The friction of which we have been speaking, is the friction called 
into play by the rubbing of two substances against each other ; the 
roughness of substances, however, exerts force to interrupt the production 
of relative motion also in the case when one body is urged to roll along 
another without rubbing; this may be called the friction of cohesion, 
depending probably upon the mutual tenacity of the two bodies. This 
species of friction was first noticed by Bossut, and afterwards carefully 
investigated by Ximenes and Coulomb : in the case of a cylinder rolling 
along a plane, the friction of cohesion is found to vary inversely as the 
diameter." 

Now were the resistance here spoken of due to cohesion, then would 
this cohesion also offer a resistance, when any force was applied to lift the 
cylinder vertically off the plane; but I am not aware that any such 
resistance is (except between particular substances) found to exist. 
Moreover, such cohesion,. if it did exist, could not offer any resistance 
to the rotatory motion of the cylinder, except under circumstances in 
which its assistance is no longer required to account for the observed 
phenomena : for if the cylinder and plane, when brought into contact, 
retained their perfect forms, the line of contact would have no finite breadth, 
and the cohesion, however great, could have no moment about this line, 
and could not consequently have any effect upon the rotation of the 
cylinder. If however we allow, as is actually the case, that the parts in 
contact are compressed, so that there is in fact a plane of contact of finite 
breadth, then the phenomena of observation are sufficiently accounted for 
without the assistance of cohesion.

CIV. Mr. G.JF, Beam, E,M. College. 

Find the moments of inertia of the five regular solids about any axis 
through their centres of gravity. 

[FiBST Solution. Mr. G. W. Ream, the Proposer."] 

Lemma. Let A, B, C be the moments of inertia of any of the five regular 
solids about principal axes through the centre ; then A, B, C are all equal, 
and consequently the moments about all other axes through the centre are 
also equal. 

For wherever the principal axes be situated, it is evidently possible to 
find other axes similarly situated with respect to the figure about which the 
moments are the same as A, B, or C, even supposing A, B, and C to be all 
dififerent. If possible therefore let A l&gt; B and B &gt; C, and let a, /3, \ be the 
angles of direction of another axis, about which the moment is C. Then by 
the usual formulae 

A cos'^a+B cos^^+C cos*7 = C, 

C08^a4-C0S^^+C0s'*7 = 1, 

.-. (A — C)cos*a+(B — C)cos*/3 = .(1). 

But since by hypothesis A&gt; C and B&gt;&gt; C ; A — C and B — C are each 
positive, and equation (1) cannot have place. Hence the hypothesis of the 
inequality of A, B, and C leads to an absurdity. Therefore A, B, and C 
are not unequal, that is, they are equal. 

Pkop. I. To find the moment of inertia of a right pyramid whose base is 
any plane regular figure, about an axis inclined at an angle a to the axis of 
the pyramid, and passing through its vertex. 

Take a section at the distance a? from the vertex parallel to the base 
and suppose the pyramid to consist of laminae of thickness da^. The square 
of the radius of gyration of the lamina at the distance a may be represented 
by ex^y e being a constant, and the mass of the lamina = fix^dx^ /* being 
another constant. Hence the moment of inertia of the lamina about the axis 
of the pyramid = epiX^dx. Also its moment of inertia about an axis in 
its own plane through the centre = \eiix^dx^ by the well known dynamical 
formulae applicable to plane figures. 

Suppose a plane to pass through the axis of the pyrapiid and the axis 
through the vertex about which the moment is required, this plane will cut 
the lamina in a line through its centre, and the moment of inertia of the 
lamina about this line as an .axis will be ^efix*dx ; and hence the moment 
about an axis parallel to this last and passing through the vertex of the 
P3rramid is 

fix^dx.x^ 4" lefix'^dx = fix*dx{\ + ^e). 

Hence by the usual formula, the moment of inertia of the lamina about the 
given axis through the vertex

Prop. ii. To deduce from the former the moment of inertia of any of the 
iive regular solids. 

These figures may all be divided into pyramids such as in the preceding 
proposition, having their common vertex in the centre of the figure, and their 
several bases the several plane faces of the figure. The axes of such 
pyramids will make diJSerent angles with an axis through the centre of which 
we consider a as the general representative. 

Then since there are as many pyramidal axes as plane faces, if n be their 
number, we have for the sum of the moments of the pyramids, or moment of 
the regular figure of n plane faces, 

4 Pa'{«(l -+■ \e)—{\ — \e)^ cos^aj ; 

but by the lemma this moment is independent of a, and therefore S cos^a is 
independent of a. This circumstance enables us to assume an axis through 
the centre most convenient for the calculation of 2 cos^a in each case. Now 
the finding of 2; cos^a is a purely spherical problem which may be stated 
thus : 

Let any of the five regular solids be inscribed in a sphere, and let any 
other point be chosen on the sphere, and let a, )3, 7, etc, be the angular 
distances of the assumed point from the several angular points of the figure, 
it is required to find the sum 

cos'a + cos'^ + cos'7 + ^^^&gt; or 2 cos^a. 

I find that if n be the number of angular points, we have 2 cos'a = -^, but 

o 
as I believe the proposition to be entirely new, I do not here give the demon- 
stration of it, intending to ofier it to the consideration of your correspondents. 
If it is not new, probably a demonstration already exists. 

The five regular solids are connected in the following simple manner. 

If perpendiculars be drawn to the plane faces of a tetrahedron they will 
intersect the surface of a concentric sphere in points which are the vertices 
of another tetrahedron. 

If a hexahedron be so treated an octahedron will be formed, and 
conversely. 

If a dodecahedron be so treated an icosahedron will be formed, and 
conversely. 

In all cases then the number of points or terms in 2 cos'a will be the same 
as the number of pyramidal axes, or the same as the number of plane faces, 

and therefore = n. Putting 2 cos^a = -;r in the formula, it becomes 
Lvna'[2{\ \\e)--[\ -\e)] 

= — Ma'( 1 + e\ where M = «P. 

That is, the moment of inertia of any of the five regular solids about an 
axis through its centre is represented by 

|Ma\l+c),

where M is its mass, a the radius of the inscribed sphere, and ^ = — , where 

k is the radius of gyration of one of its plane faces about an axis through its 
centre perpendicular to its plane.

for all the five solids, as this is easily accomplished, and shall content myself 
with the following table of their values.

CVI. Mr. Dames. 

A complete spherical quadrilateral and its three diagonals are formed of 
great circles, and through the extremities of each diagonal and the inter- 
sections of the other two, other great circles are drawn : it is required to 
prove, independently of projective considerations, that all the circles concerned 
in the figure are so divided by each other, that the sines of their segments are 
in harmonical proportion. 

{Pemonatration hy the Proposer.'] 

1. Definition of the figure. Let O be the centre of the sphere upon 
which the simple salient quadrilateral ABCD is described ; all the circles 



332 



SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXBBCI8S8. 



here spoken of being Understood as great circles of the sphere. Let £ and 
F be the intersections of the opposite sides of the quadrilateral; viz. of 
AB, CD and of BC, AD : and draw AC, BD, EF intersecting respectively 
two and two in P, Q, R as in the figure. Also, draw the circles EP, FP 
meeting the sides of the quadrilateral ABCD in G, U, K, L ; and complete 
the several great circles now specified, upon the surface of the sphere. 

Now for every intersection of two circles of this figure (as A, for 
instance) there will be a second ( A') made by the intersection of the same 
two circles AB, AD diametrically opposite to it on the spherical surface. 
For the purpose of more readily 
distinguishing the parts of the 
fii^ure by the eye, the parts on 
the two hemispheres separated 
by the circle EFE'F' are mark- 
ed in plain and in dotted line ; 
and eveiy point diametrically 
opposite to a point already de- 
scribed is marked by the same 
letter accented. 

Then the entire figure formed 
by the four circles AB, BC, 
CD, DA being completed so as 
to intersect each other mutually 
in E, E, F', F, A', B, C',D\ 
A, B,C,I) constitutes the com- 
plete spherical quadrilateral; 
and the three great circles AC, 
BD, EF being completed, con- 
stitute its three diagonals. The circles EP, FP are specified in the enun- 
ciation of the property ; but they have received no special name. 

2. Preliminary properties of the figure. Reference might be made 
to Carnot's Essai sur les Transver sales* ^ to a paper of my own in Ley- 
bourn's Repository, vol. vi.f , or to the second volume of my edition of 
Hutton*6 CourseJ, for some of these : but to avoid the scattered reference 
to works which might not be in the hands of every reader, it is deemed 
advisable to give them here, so as to render the entire investigation 
complete from beginning to end. 

{a,) If a spJierical triangle ABD be cut by a transversal in E, F, Q, 
fve shall have the following relation amongst its segments : — 

sin AF sin BE sin DQ = sin AE sin BQ sin DF. 

For by a fundamental property of the spherical triangles AFE, BEQ, 
FQD, we have 

sin AF : sin AE :: sinE : sin F, 

sin BE : sin BQ :: sin Q : sin E, 

sin DQ : sin DF :: sin F : sin Q ; 

and multiplying, etc., we get the equation as above stated 




or 



In the figure, the three sides are cut in their extensions in E, F, Q (ur 
E', F, Q'): but the demonstration is equally applicable if only one of the 



* Page 86. 



t Page C7. 



t Page 78. 



SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISS8. 333 

sides be cut in its extension. This will be seen at once by constructing the 
figure to correspond to the case ; or, which is nearly the same thing, by 
supposing the triangle EAF to be cut by the transversal in B, D, Q, and 
then forming the requisite proportions. It will, moreover, in some cases, be 
necessary to substitute the sine of the supplement for the sine of the angle 
or the arc concerned ; which being always of the same value is perfectly 
allowable in a research which relates to values only. 

(h) If through any point C on the spherical surface and the angular 
points A, B, D, of a spherical triangle^ transoersals he drawn to cut 
the sides AB, BD, DA (produced or not as the position of C may 
require) in P, E, F ; then the following relations amongst the segments 
will subsist : — 

sin AF sin BE sin DP = sin AE sin BP sin DF. 

For the triangle ABD is divided into two others ABP, APD by the trans- 
versal AC : and the triangle ABP is cut by the transversal DE in D, C, E ; 
and APD by BF in B, C, F. Whence, by the preceding property, we have 

sin BD sin PC sin AE = sin DP sin CA sin EB, 
sin BP sin CA sin FD = sin DB sin PC sin AF ; 
and multiplying these we find the stated property to result. 

The same remarks as to the extensions of the sides of the triangles, and 
the substitution of supplements for the arcs themselves, apply here as in the 
preceding case. Carnot, however, limits the point C to be within the field 
of the triangle ABD, which is an unnecessary and inconvenient restriction*. 
The method of investigation is, however, entirely due to that distinguished 
geometer. 

(c.) het an arc AB of a great circle he divided in C and D so that 

sin AC : sin CB :: sin AD : sin DB ; 

also let arcs he drawn from A, B, C, D to any point P on the sphere^ 

and these be cut hy another spherical transversal in A', B', C, D': 

theny 

sin AC : sin C'B' :: sin A'D ' : D'B'. 
(ITie reader will readiljf sketch the figure,) 
For, 

sin AC : sin CP :: sin A PC : sin PAC = sin PAD, 
sin BD : sin DP :: sin BPD : sin PBD = sin PBC, 
sin CP : sin CB :: sin PBC : sin CPB, 
sin DP : sin DA :: sin PAD : sin APD. 
Compounding which, we obtain 

sin AC sin BD : sin CB sin AD :: sin APC sin BPD : sin CPB sin APD ; 
or, taking account of the hypothesis, this becomes 

sin APC sin BPD = sin CPB sin APD. 

* It is singular that Gamot (having conceived, and so efficiently used, the idea of the eom- 
plete qttadrilateral inplano) should not have considered the complete spherical triariffle : viz. 
that figure which is formed of the entire circles, the three segments of which (one segment of 
each circle) form the ordinary objects of spherical trigonometry. No general researches into 
the properties of spherical triangles can he complete without taking into consideration the 
irhole system of these circles ; or what I have ventured elsewhere to call the ** associated 
system" and its symmetrical system. With Carnot, however, the properties of spherical 
figures were only ohjects of investigation incidentally ; and he does not appear to have 
attempted any researches respecting them except so far as some direct analogy with plane 
triangles might have suggested any spherical property to his mind, 

TJtr 



334 SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXEBCI8KS. 

By the nature of the inquiry, also, the hypothesis and conclusioa are con- 
vertible ; hence the converse is also true : viz. that if 

sin APC : sin CPB :: sin APD : sin BPD, 
then also, 

sin AC : sin CB :: sin AD : sin DB, 

wherever the transversal AB may be taken so as to cut the four arcs 
AP, BP, CP, DP ; and hence when they are taken in the position A'B'. 

Whence the property is true. 

3. Demonstration of the properties of the complete quadrikUeral. 

From {a) and (b) we have 

sin DQ : sin QB :: sin AE sin DF : sin EB sin FA, 

sin DP : sin PB :: sin AE sin DF : sin EB sin FA ; 
whence it follows that 

sin DQ : sin QB :: sin DP : sin PB. 

That is, the diagonal DB of the spherical quadrilateral ABCD is harmo- 
nically divided in P and Q. 

In the same way it is shewn that the diagonal AC is harmonically divided 
in P and R, and FE in R and Q. 

Again, since DB is harmonically divided in P and Q, and arcs are drawn 
from each point of division to F ; and that the arcs DC and AB are trans- 
versal to the arcs FD, FP, FB, FQ, they are themselves divided harmo- 
nically in K, E and L, E. Similarly AD and BC are harmonically divided 
in E, F and H, F. So, likewise are GH and KL divided harmonically, as 
stated in the enunciation. 

It is obvious that in this sense of the term ^' harmonical division of an 
arc" we may substitute for any arc its supplement: and thus shew the 
relation to subsist, mutatis mutandis, amongst all the parts of the complete 
quadrilateral. The inquiry is now reduced to too simple a state to need 
any further detail in a work like this. 

Scholium — A great number of properties — in fact all which have any 
analogy to the complete plane quadrilateral — will follow at once from 
the preceding investigation. For instance Brianchon's fasceau harmo- 
nique — the properties of inscribed and circumscribed quadrilaterals in 
reference to a spherical conic section — Pascal's mystic hexagram — and 
indeed, all that relates to Poles and Polars on t?ie sphere. As far, in fact, 
as is yet known, this method is the only one by which spherical properties 
can be completely investigated ; and perhaps the transversal on the sphere 
may ultimately prove to be the perfect substitute for the similar triangles in 
piano, when we enter into analogous spherical researches. This, however, 
is only to be understood in reference to geometrical methods in piano and not 
to the coordinate methods of investigation. On the sphere, the coordinate 
methods are as immediately applicable as in piano : but this topic is already 
in good hands — in the hands of the Editors of the " Mathematician," under 
the form of ** poles and polars" and the '* collinear theory." 

I may add that this proposition was sent to Professor Gill of New 
York, with whom I was in correspondence on other subjects, several years 
ago ; and that (whether from his oversight or mine, I am not able to say) it 
was proposed in an imperfect form in his '' Mathematical Miscellany" as an 
exercise. It was pronounced by the American geometers, most truly, that 
in the form in which it was proposed, no such property existed ; and the 
discontinuance of that valuable periodical prevented my correction of it 



SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 335 

through that medium. I, however, in my edition of Hutton*s Course enun- 
ciated the theorem in reference to the figure here employed (p. 83) ; and 
by the kindness of the editors of the Mathematician have now been enabled 
to publish the demonstration. 




CVII. W.Burns, LLD,, Rochester. 

Given the vertical angle of a triangle, the ratio of the radii of the circles 
inscribed in the two triangles formed by the line bisecting the vertical angle 
and meeting the base, and the distance between the centres of these circles, 
to construct the triangle. 
[First Solution. Messrs. W.H.Levyy Shalboum; WiUiam Mortimer , SeecRey, 

near Manchester ; Nicholas Smythy Ckdway; and Dr. BumSy the proposer.] 

On AB, the given distance between the centres of tjie inscribed circles, 
describe a segment of a circle containing an angle equal to half the given 
vertical angle ; divide AB in D in the given ratio of the radii of the inscribed 
circles ; through D and the middle point of the arc AB draw a line meeting 
the circumference in G, and draw 
AF, BE perpendicular to DG. 
From A and B with the radii AF 
and BE respectively describe cir- 
cles touching DG ; then from G 
draw the tangents GH and GK, 
and also HE, a common tangent 
to both circles, meeting the former 
in H and K; then GHK is the 
triangle required. 

For draw AG and BG ; then 
the angle AGD is equal to the 
angle BGD, since they stand on equal circumferences, by construction ; and 
since angle AGD is equal to angle AGH, and BGD to BGE, the whole angle 
HGE is evidently double of the angle AGB, and therefore equal to the given 
angle. Again, by similar triangles ADF, and BDE, AD : BD :: AF : BE ; 
therefore the radii of the inscribed circles are in the given ratio, and AB is 
the given distance between the centres. 

Scholium. By di*awing the other common tangent to the two inscribed 
circles, the preceding construction and demonstration would then apply to 
the problem : — 

Given the vertical angle, the ratio of the radii of the circles described 
without the triangle, each touching the base, a side, and the line bisecting 
the vertical angle ; and the distance between the centres of these circles, to 
construct the triangle.' 

[Second Solution. Messrs. Hugh God/ray, St. MarKs Road, Jersey ; and 
David Mavevy Bon Accord School, Aberdeen.'] 
Draw any two lines G A and GB forming an angle equal to half the given 
angle. On these take two distances GM and GN proportional* to the two 
radii, and join MN, on which produced, if necessary, setoff MO equal to the 
given distance of the centres. Draw OB parallel to GA, meeting GB in 
B ; and also BA parallel to MO. Bisect the angle AGB by the line GL, 
and with centres A and B describe circles to touch GL. Draw the 
tangents GH and GK and the double tangent HK ; then HGK shall be 
the triangle required. 



SdO SOLtTTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXEB0I8ES. 

For draw the radii AF and BE to the points of contact of the circles with 
GL ; then the similar triangles GAF and GBE give 

AF : BE :: GA : GB :: GM : GN ; 
therefore AF has to BE the given ratio ; also AB is equal to MO, the given 
distance, and angle HGK is equal to twice the angle AGB, and therefore 
equal to the given angle. 

[Third Solution. Mr. Joseph Pauly Shooter's HiU.] 
Since the geometrical construction of Exercise 108 is included in the 
solution of the problem of which this is a particular case, namely, that in 
which the vertical angle is divided into any two parts; I shall give a 
solution of the more general problem. 

Let the given distance AB of the centres be divided in D in the given 
ratio of the radii, and let the angles CAB and CBA be the halves of the 
given parts of the vertical angle ; through A, C and B describe the circle 
ABG ; join CD, and produce it to meet the circumference in G ; draw the 
perpendiculars AF and BE, with which as radii describe circles; the tan- 
gents GH and GK, and the common tangent (see Bland's Geo. Pr., 
sect. 2nd. prob. 7,) HK of those circles shall be the sides of the triangle. 

Join GA and GB : then the angles AGC and BGC are respectively equal 
to ABC and BAC, being in the same segment ; therefore their doubles, 
namely, HGL and KGL, are the given parts of the given angle ; it is also 
manifest that AF : BE :: AD : BD, the given ratio of the radii. 

Analytical solutions of this exercise were received from H. W. and E. W., of King's College, London.

[Solution. W, Burns, L. L. D,, St. Margarets Bank, Hochester ; and similarly by 
Mr. Harley, the proposer.]

1 1 8. Mr, S. Barry, City School, London. 
Given the base of a triangle, and the angle subtended by it at the centre 
of gravity of the triangle ; to find the locus of the vertex. 

119. Mr. A. B Clarke, Gentleman Cadet, B. M. Academy. 
Let straight lines be drawn through the angular points of a plane triangle 
ABC perpendicular to the lines bisecting these angles ; if A, P be the area 
and perimeter of the original triangle, and Aj, P^ those of the new triangle, 
then (1) 4A.Ai = P.a5c 

(2) P.Pi = 4Ai(cos |A+co8 p+cos |C) 

120. Mr. John Taylor, Woolwich Common. 
Let ABC be a plane triangle ; DEF the triangle formed by joining the 
points where perpendiculars from the angles A, B, C meet the opposite 
sides ; O the centre of the circumscribed circle, and R its radius ; P the 
intersection of the perpendiculars ; and p the radius of the circle inscribed in 
DEF ; then there exists the following relation, 

AP* + BP' + CP'* + OP'^ = 5 R' ± 8R^, 
the upper or lower sign being taken according as the triangle ABC is obtuse 
or acute. 

121. Mr. J. J. Walker, Trinity College, Dublin. 
Let ABC, ADC be two isosceles triangles on the base AC, and on the 
same side of it, having their vertical angles as 3 : 2. Let be the centre of 
the circle circumscribing the triangle ABC: draw the diameter BOE ; in OBD 
produced take BF = BO ; and let the arcs Aw, A» be respectively one 
third and tm&gt; thirds of the arc A£ : prove that

124. Mr, Thomas Weddle, J\rewcastle, 
A straight line is given in position, and the axes of an ellipse both in position

125. By the same Gentleman, 
The sum of the squares of the reciprocals of the areas of the sections of an 
ellipsoid made by any three diametral planes at right angles to each other, is 
equal to the sum of the squares of the reciprocals of the areas of the sections 
made by the three principal planes.

127. By J. J. 
A quantity of electricity diffuses itself over the surface of a given ellipsoid :

128. By Calculus.

in length, aiid rises 1 in 75. Now admitting locomotive resistance when the

the commencement of the descent.
